*The VA Magazine* are the opinion  of the authors. The views reflected  do not necessarily reflect the views  

We all know that running your own business is exciting, fulfilling  

& inspiring. It is also at times scary, lonely and frustrating. 

As your own boss, working on your own, when you encounter  

problems or come up against a situation you are unsure about, there  

is often nobody to bounce ideas off or run your solutions by. 

The last two months have been a good example of this for me.  

When it’s just you, other things in your life impact and where there  

is no back up in place, if you are not careful the house of cards can  

seem in danger of crashing down about your ears. 

Four lessons learned 

1)  Don’t take on too much. It can be tempting to say yes to every  

opportunity thrown at you and then find yourself overwhelmed  

and exhausted. There are only 24 hours a day and you do need  

to sleep sometimes 

2)  If you need help, ask for it. If you are not an expert in something or there is a task that you really don’t  enjoy, it is better to pay someone else to do it and get on with the bits that you love. Life is short and  whilst you are busy doing the things you don’t like, the things you excel at are passing you by. 

3)  Get backup in place so that if there is a family emergency or technological problem, you have someone  else in place who can go into gear and get it done for you so you don’t let your clients down. 

4)  Networking is king. Getting out and meeting groups of other business people both online and in  person is a lifesaver both from the point of view of contacts but also for realising that everyone goes  through the same issues and they are surmountable.  

So in the end, the house of cards turned out to be solid and building on rock not sand. Equilibrium and sleep  have been restored & I hope to meet lots of you at the many events taking place in the next few months. 

Here’s to all of you bravely running the gauntlet every day. The scary bits pass quickly and the excitement  and inspiration return as you realise that whether it works or not is down to you. You are in control of your  destiny. 

**Lucy Brazier, Editor ![][image1]**

*Cover Feature* 

**How Selling Your VA  Services Doesn’t Have To  Be Quite So Painful** 

WRITER   
Gary   
Gorman 

So, you’ve just set up your VA business. You know you’re good.  You know you’re efficient. You know you can help lots of people  become more efficient themselves. All you need to do now is tell  people just how good you are.

four *www\.thevamagazine.com* 

You go out networking because that’s what  your local Chamber of Commerce contact said  you should do. You get business cards and flyers  printed, along with a glossy brochure. You set  up your website. 

And you are greeted with, let’s face it, general  indifference\! 

The world, contrary to what you thought, is not  exactly beating a path to your door. People are  sceptical about what you can do for them, they  show some interest but this goes when they get  back to their day job. They may say: ‘I’ll think  about it,’ leaving you waiting for them to call  or fretting about when is the best time to call  them because you don’t want to seem too pushy  or desperate. 

Eventually things drift and you’re frustrated  because you know you can help them. 

Have you ever considered that you might be  selling too much to them? Selling to them so  much that they back off because nobody likes  being sold to and certainly not in an obvious  way. People can smell desperation and nobody  wants to buy from desperate people because  desperate people can’t have many clients and if  they don’t have many clients they can’t be any  good, can they? 

Would a better way help you? A way that  encourages more people to buy from you more  often. 

This article will help you to do just that.  These are my top tips to make selling your  VA services less painful for both you and your  potential clients. 

**1\. Don’t tell people what you do, tell  them why it matters.** 

I come across lots of VAs who say things like  ‘I’m a whizz at Wordpress’ or ‘I can help you  manage your diary better’ or ‘I can set up a  website for you.’ 

That’s all well and good but all the client wants  to know is what’s in it for them, what is the  benefit? 

Typical benefits a VA may provide are: 

*•  Reassurance* that the job will get done on  time  

•  They *free up* time enabling the client to get  on with what they’re good at 

*•  Speed* by completing tasks quicker than the  client could do 

*•  Less hassle* by getting the job done right  first time   
*Cover Feature* 

I’m sure you can come up with many more  benefits that you provide through your services  (why not take a piece of paper and brainstorm  them now?) That is what people will really be  buying from you: what you help them achieve  or avoid. How you do it is not particularly  interesting to them. 

**2\. Be consistent** 

Once you have clarified in your own mind  why what you do really matters to the benefit  seeking client, tell them all about it.  

Plaster the top three benefits all over your  business card, your website, your stationery \-  even get them on your invoices if you can\! 

Practice a punchy 40 seconds benefit statement  that you can deliver convincingly at a  networking meeting. Remember it’s not what  you do but why it matters at this stage. 

Deliver the message over and over again. A  confused customer will never buy from you  so keep telling them your three key benefits  in plain and simple language. A consistent  message makes it easier for them to refer you  to their contacts and the only way you’ll get  this is if it’s clear what you help clients gain  or avoid. 

**3\. Don’t sell, listen first.** 

In order for someone to want to buy your VA  services from you they need to be convinced  that you understand what they need. You won’t  get there by selling to them but you will get  there by listening to them. And I mean really  listening, without interruptions or jumping to  conclusions. 

The four things you are trying to find out  initially are: 

**•  Where are they now?**  

What is the issue holding them back or the  next opportunity they want to take? 

**•  Where do they want to be?** 

What is their goal? Where do they want to get  to? How quickly do they want to get there? 

**•  Where are the gaps?** 

In what way does their future objective differ  from the current reality? 

**•  How can you help them?** 

What skills, knowledge or expertise do you  have that can help them get to where they want  to be? 

You will only ever get through these four  questions by really listening to what they say  without pre-judging the outcome. They may  

soon be buying from you so they deserve to be  listened to. 

**4\. Approach networking in the same way** 

Go to networking meetings to listen not  necessarily to sell. Be liked first, people tend to  buy from those that they like and people like  those that look them in the eye and listen to  them.  

Be liked, be trusted, be clear on the benefits  you offer and the sale or onward referral will  come. 

**5\. Stand out from the crowd** 

The VA market is crowded isn’t it? There are  lots of people doing what you do. Maybe not in  exactly the same way but ultimately delivering  what you deliver. 

So the challenge is to stand out. But how? 

Well just by simply and clearly communicating  the benefits you provide rather than the features  you will stand out already. Believe me, there  are so many VAs who get this wrong so you’ll  definitely stand out. 

Get a distinctive business card using a different  size or material from the norm, maybe using  a quirky image or photo of you rather than a  generic logo. 

Maybe dress differently or have a colour theme.  I know someone who always has something  visible in pink, either a piece of her clothing or  a pen or notebook. Always there and always in  pink. It works. Try it. 

I also know someone who goes to her business  networking meetings carrying a shabby old  Tesco shopping bag stuffed to overflowing with  receipts. She stands up and says ‘Does your  book-keeping system look like this? If it does  I can help you sort it out, leaving you time to  get out and see more customers.’ I’ve seen that  work incredibly well as both an eye-catching  visual prop and as a great way of standing out  from other book-keepers. 

Be brave, be different, stand out\! 

**6\. Turbo charge your testimonials** 

One of the biggest challenges you’ll face when  trying to get a new customer is their scepticism.  At first they will be thinking ‘Is this person as  good as they seem \- do I believe them?’ 

Testimonials from your current clients will  really help you to overcome this natural  scepticism. They must be specific, saying what  a nice person you are may flatter the ego but it 

*www\.thevamagazine.com* five 

won’t make a great deal of difference to a new  client. They want to know what life was like  before you were hired and the impact afterwards  (preferably with some figures to back it up). A  great testimonial might be something like this: 

*‘Before I worked with Susan I really felt unsure  how to manage the mountain of e-mails I receive  each week. Now Susan has helped me to organize my  e-mail management efficiently I can get it all sorted  out in ten minutes at the start of the day. This has  gained me about three hours each week that I can put  to better use.’* 

This works well because it’s specific in terms of  time saved, it’s emotional (‘I felt unsure’) and  it’s based around a before and after comparison. 

**7\. Make your sales copy really work** 

Every piece of writing you do to grow your  business should have a sales objective. If not it’s  just a hobby and you’re an unpaid author. 

Let’s think about your newsletter. How many  newsletters do you receive that say: ‘Read my  latest newsletter’ or ‘Newsletter April edition’.  Inspiring? I doubt you’ll be rushing to read it? 

So what about if you read: ‘The five things you  must do to get more customers’ or ‘I bet you’re  making this mistake in your business’. Both  statements provoke a feeling of curiosity and  are much more likely to get your newsletter  read. 

If you want to get your copy read more often  have an attention grabbing headline. Then  back up that interest with a few questions in  the first paragraph, think about the reader and  how they might be feeling: 

‘Are you frustrated at being constantly beaten  down on price?’ 

‘Do you wish you had more hours in the day to  get through your workload?’ 

*about the author*   
*Cover Feature* 

‘Do you wonder how to get your website really  generating ££££?’ 

This gets the reader engaged and in a state of  agreement \- maybe even nodding as they read.  Once they are inquisitive they are more likely  to read the rest of the copy. 

After you’ve got them interested then you can  deliver the sales promise. Use short sentences  and simple words, remember that you’re not  trying to show off your extensive vocabulary or  high level of grammar. Your purpose is to sell.  Writing copy that sells and words that impress  are two very different things. 

Add your testimonials and then the call to  action, by this I mean what exactly do you want  them to do as a result of reading your copy: do  you want them to subscribe to your newsletter?  Do you want them to call you? Do you want  them to download a report? Whatever it is tell  them to do it in simple language. 

**8\. Ask them to take the next step** 

I come across many VAs who don’t want to  appear too pushy when it comes to asking for  the client’s business so they either ask clumsily  and obviously or not at all. 

I think the best way to get a decision in a  natural, conversational way is to ask: 

*‘So, what do you see as the next step?’* 

At best they will say ‘When can you start?’  At the very least it will tell you what needs  to happen before they are willing to go ahead.  It gives them authority and ownership of the  next stage and stops you feeling awkward about  asking for the business. Try it. It works really  well. 

**9\. Stay positive** 

In any business, particularly when you work  on your own, it can be very easy to get down  

when business is slow but keeping a positive  outlook is vital for ultimate success. To add a  bit of ‘confidence dust maybe try to bear a few  things in mind: 

•  Some people in this world will like you.  Some people will not like you. That’s  simply just the way it is. 

•  Some days rubbish happens. It just does.  Move on from it. Tomorrow will be better. •  Celebrate your successes, however small.  It’s what keeps you going and focused. •  Nothing happens by luck. It is almost  always as a result of your previous hard  work. You create your own good fortune  and you deserve it. 

•  You can only control how you react to  other people and events, not how others  react. So only work on the bits you can  control. 

•  Sometimes people won’t buy no matter  how good your offer is. They just won’t.  Move on to those that will. 

I hope I’ve helped to show you that selling  your VA services doesn’t have to be painful  or difficult. Listen more than you talk. Think  ultimately of the benefits you provide rather  than how you do it. Be different. Be brave. Ask  them how they want to go forward. 

Trust me…you can do it\! 

Good luck and once you get your sales going  up, up, up maybe you could let me know if this  article helped. 

Gary Gorman is a leading sales mentor, trainer and business speaker. He helps businesses attract the right type of clients  and then successfully sell to them and convert them into loyal customers. He is the author of two highly rated books:  *The 30 Day Sales Challenge* and *Inside The Entrepreneur’s Mind*.  

For more information on how Gary could help your business grow visit *www\.GaryGorman.co.uk* or  

call him on 01476 516282\.

*Personal Development* 

**What Would I Do If I  Were A New VA Today?** 

In some ways I’m envious of VAs starting out today because there  is so much more available than when I began nearly 19 years ago.  But that choice can make it hard to focus and determine what  really needs to be done. 

WRITER   
Kathie Thomas 

So, what would I do if I were a new VA today?  This was the question asked on a VA forum and  my answers are based on my own experience  and knowledge in running a Virtual Assistant  business. 

•  Find an accountant who specialises  in supporting small businesses and  ensure that you meet the right legal  requirements for your business. They  need to be tax experts who can advise you  on what is claimable in running an online  business. 

•  Apply for whatever licenses or register  whatever you need to do legally regarding  the tax department for your region. Ask  questions and do research. Ignorance is  not bliss in this case and you need to know  what you need to know. 

•  A business name can wait until you are  trading – it is not necessary and you can  legally operate under your own name if  you wish. Having a business name is nice  but too many people hold off getting  started because they just can’t come up  with the right name. Mine came out of  writing a brochure telling people about  my new business. Yours might come  unexpectedly too. 

•  Get your business cards made – you can  just order a few hundred. You must have  something to give to people you meet  face-to-face. Include: your name, contact  details and a bit about what you do. It’s  better than not having something to give  out when meeting prospective clients. 

*about the author* 

*![]()*•  Purchase a domain name – these are low  cost and one that reflects your name is fine  for now. If you later decide on a business  

name you can buy a new domain and get  that pointing to your website as well. •  Set up a website – there are easy low  cost options for doing this such as using  WordPress or Joomla. Make sure you can  use it for business and steer away from  sites that have advertising all over them.  You need to look professional even if you  have a low budget. Low cost web hosting  will allow you to have WordPress set up  on your own domain. 

•  Develop a signature block to use  online – it’s like your online business card.  It doesn’t have to be very long, two or  three lines are sufficient but use the space\! 

Don’t rush to choose your business name, logo  or other things that could change six months  

later when you have a better idea of what you  want to do and where you’re headed. I know  that I changed my mind many times about the  type of services I wanted to provide and the  type of client I wanted to meet. 

When you’ve been in business for a while you’ll  get a feel for what you like doing and what you  don’t and from there you might start deciding  upon a niche or set of services you prefer to  provide. At that point a business name may  well come to you. 

Finally, find business networks in your area so  you can meet other local businesses. This will  have two particular benefits: 

1\. You can learn from others who have been  in business in your region for a while and  they will know how things work locally.  

2\. You will meet potential clients. Most  business owners need some sort of admin  assistance. And many people will do  business with people they’ve met and like. 

You can also locate online business networks  for the same reason. LinkedIn is great but  there are many others. Some have email  based forums, others have board type forums.  Don’t stick with one network – find a few.  Peer/industry based forums, forums based on  personal interests and hobbies, general business  forums. It is the same with local networks, mix  it up so that you get a variety of contacts and  experiences. Networking has always been one  of my main sources of finding clients – it can  be yours too. 

Kathie M. Thomas is a successful Virtual Assistant with an 18+ year old practice. She was a VA before the term ‘Virtual  Assistant’ was in use. The founder of A Clayton’s Secretary, established March 1994, she pioneered the Virtual Assistant  industry in Australia. The network was formally established in April 1996\. Prior to establishing her business she had  a background of 23 years secretarial and administrative experience in the corporate world, working for CEOs and MDs  as well as project groups. Kathie also has experience as a Trainer and Coach and began her business teaching others how  to use Microsoft Office products in 1994\. Kathie has two accreditations in the Virtual Assistant Industry as a Master  Virtual Assistant and an Accredited Secretary Online. You can find her at *http\://www\.vadirectory.net*

*www\.thevamagazine.com* seven   
*Marketing* 

WRITER   
Tawnya **Boost Your Sales With** Sutherland **Affiliate Marketing**  

As a Virtual Assistant, your main revenue stream will be selling your services  online to your prospective target market. Selling your services is hard work\! It  requires a constant marketing plan that you must always be working at to find  new clients. Servicing clients makes up the bulk of our revenue and without it  we would be out of business.

eight   
*www\.thevamagazine.com*   
*Marketing* 

*![]()*  
**Or would we?** 

Working as a Virtual Assistant you will find  that there will always be down times when  clients just aren’t steadily flowing into our sales  funnel. So, what plan do you have in place for  extra income during these phases when you  find you have a lack of clients?  

This is the time to bring affiliate marketing  into your marketing arsenal and if you aren’t  having a down time, adding affiliate marketing  to your online marketing plan can be an easy  source of extra money.  

**What is affiliate marketing?** 

Affiliate marketing basically means selling  other people’s products for a commission online.  It is similar to offline sales, where sales people  earn a commission for selling a product, but the  difference is that all selling is conducted online.  

As an affiliate marketer you will research and  find products to promote on your website that  are targeted to your market. For example, if you  have prospective online business entrepreneurs  visiting your site to outsource work to your  VA business, you might have a resources page  of other people’s products that would benefit  them such as: accounting software, security  products, newsletter services and marketing  products 

Each time you make a sale through a click on  an affiliate link at your website you will earn a  portion of that sale. The commissions can vary  from as low as 7% to a generous 50%. 

**How are these sales tracked?** The sales are  tracked through your unique affiliate ID which  is assigned to you at the time you sign up for  a specific affiliate programme. Your unique  affiliate ID is tracked and each time a sale is  made through your link it is recorded.  

**Your main affiliate link will look  something like this:**  

**h t t p : / / w w w. v a ff i l i a t e s . c o m / c m d . php?af=XXXXXX** 

(with **XXXXXX** being your distinct affiliate  ID so we can track your sales).  

A good affiliate programme will give you  plenty of tools for promoting their products  from banner ads and sales copy, to Tweets and  Facebook posts. 

They will also give you a comprehensive  member’s area where you can track your affiliate  sales and commissions. This allows you to  watch which marketing campaigns are selling  for you and gives you statistics on your payable  commissions for sales from your website. 

Affiliate marketing is a good way to start  making extra money online and you can find  programmes online by searching for topics such  as “marketing” and adding the words “affiliate  programme”. You can be set up and ready to  go within a few days and there is no need to  spend any time creating your own products  or purchasing inventory. You are simply  promoting other people’s products, the vendor    
is responsible for order fulfillment, customer  service and anything else related to the sale. 

Once you have added the links and banners to  your website don’t just sit back and wait for  the affiliate cheque to arrive – it does take a  bit of time and effort to make sales. A good  strategy is to provide quality information on  your website and only link to affiliate products  where appropriate. I never promote something  that I haven’t used. 

Another tip is to review the products you  promote so that you can accurately describe all  the features – good and bad. Your customers  will appreciate honest feedback and will be  more likely to buy from you if you give them  good quality information and honest reviews. 

Don’t forget about other traffic that visits your  website, your competitors can be money in  your inbox too\! 

**Potential Clients make up about ½ your target market. Virtual Assistants make up a good portion of the rest\!**

Check your website stats regularly to see what  keywords people are searching for to visit  your website and start marketing products to  them. You’ll find it’s not just potential clients  who visit your website. Other VAs, your  competitors, will be checking out your website  to compare rates, website design ideas or just  to see what their competition is up to. consider putting up a resources page of affiliate  products for VAs? 

**Affiliate tip:** If you are a member of an  organisation and have their logo on your website,  find out if they offer an affiliate programme  and then you can add your affiliate link in the  logo itself. My network, VAnetworking.com,  offers an affiliate programme so if you have our  member logo on your website, please sign-up  to the affiliate programme at VAaffiliates.com.  By adding your affiliate link to the member  logo you can start making money today\! 

So how should you select an affiliate  programme? There is a lot of choice out there  and it can be difficult to know which are the  best to promote. Here are a few tips to help  you find the right affiliate programmes for you: 

**Lifetime cookies –** A really good affiliate  programme will offer life-time cookies.  Unfortunately, some shopping cart systems  don’t allow the vendors this feature, which  isn’t so great for you as the affiliate. A life 

time cookie means that you will get paid for  referring a customer who makes a purchase  through your affiliate link even if they buy at  any point in the future. 

When looking for a good programme, try to  lookout for those which offer life-time cookies  or at least a minimum of a year. Remember  sometimes it takes customers months to  actually make a purchase. 

**Training and Customer Service –** A good  programme will provide training tools and  resources for their affiliates. Ideally, they should  have a member’s area where you can log in and  grab promotional materials, your links and  check your stats and commissions. 

Good communication is important. If you  email an affiliate programme with a question  and don’t hear back from them or get a reply  two weeks later, what will it be like if you ever  have an issue with payments or other important  questions? A good affiliate programme will  respond to your questions in a timely manner.  

**Sales Pages That Convert –** Before promoting  a specific product, have a look at their sales  page. Does it look like it will convert into  sales? If a sales page is sloppy, has grammar and  spelling mistakes or just looks unprofessional  then you may not make too many sales – no  matter how good the product is. 

**Now that you know some of the things you  should look for in a good programme, here  are a few things you should be careful of\!**   
*Marketing* 

**Advertisements and Google Adsense –**  If you’re promoting an eBook and sending  customers to a sales page then be wary of  sales pages with Google ads or other types  of advertisement on the sales page. If you’re  working hard to send traffic to that page, it’s  not fair that your traffic will be used to make  someone else money through their ads (without  you getting paid anything in return). 

If you see a sales page with tons of ads on  the page, I say stay away. You work hard as  an affiliate and you don’t want to waste your  traffic. 

**High Sales Minimum –** Watch out for  programmes that ask you to sell a certain  amount before being paid. For example if the  commission on a £20 eBook is 10% (£2 per sale)  and you are asked to accumulate a minimum  of £200 in commission before payment is sent  then that’s a lot of books you need to sell before  getting paid – 100 books to be exact. 

Some programmes operate on the basis that  affiliates will not make many sales and so will  never be paid. It’s unfortunate but it does  happen. Always check the payment terms  before promoting a programme. 

**Affiliate marketing is not hard but it does  need firm conviction and belief in the  product.**  

If you’re looking for products to promote to  the VA industry or just want to learn more  about affiliate marketing check out my blog at:  http\://VAaffiliates.com 

So how do you implement affiliate marketing  into your marketing? I’ll give you a few  examples below but don’t forget to think of  other ways you can entice people to click on  your affiliate links and bring money into your  email box 24/7. 

**1\. Write a Blog Post:** Writing a blog post  to review the product you are promoting  is an informative way to indirectly link  to the affiliate product you recommend.  So, if you are member of VAinsider.com,  you could write a short one or two page  summary on your blog about what you  have learned. If you had gained ideas from  Tawnya about how to market your business  locally or how to create a contingency plan  then you would write a review on how this  knowledge affected your business and why  it would help others.  

Don’t forget to add the banner ad or your  affiliate text link to the end of the post to  direct people to the product. Once you  

have the blog post written then tell the  world\! Tweet about it, add it to Facebook  or send it to your mailing list. 

**2\. Video Testimonials:** Video is a powerful  medium for any type of internet  marketing promotion. Think about  buying an inexpensive video camcorder  and make your own video testimonial  about a product. It doesn’t have to be  in-depth or long, just make sure your  personality comes through and that you  are conveying the benefits of the product.  Here’s what you could say if you were  promoting my VA startup system at  www\.VirtualBusinessStartups.com: 

*“After begining my Virtual Assistant career I soon  realised that there was so much more involved than  just sitting at my computer typing documents. I was  soon introduced to the Virtual Business Start Up  System written by Tawnya Sutherland \- founder of  the largest social network online today for VAs. I  couldn’t believe how many resources were included in  this product. I was immediately able to get my VA  business up and running in less time than if I started  from scratch. I’m all about not inventing the wheel  so if you are looking at starting or moving your VA  business to a different level then consider saving your  time by purchasing the Virtual Business Start Up  System, it will be the best investment you make.“* 

**3\. Signatures:** Put affiliate links in your  email signatures as further promotion and  don’t forget to update your signature in  any forums you belong to but do check out  their rules first and make sure that they  allow affiliate signatures. 

**4\. Social Networks:** Don’t be afraid to  promote affiliate programmes through  your Tweets, Facebook and LinkedIn posts  but don’t overdo it or people will stop  following you. Mix in these status updates  with personal and business conversations.  Remember social networking is all about  engagement and no one wants to engage  with a person selling all the time. 

**5\. Give something away at your website  to encourage people to visit it.** The  more traffic you have to your website then  the better your chance of clicks to your  affiliate links. You could entice people to  your website by running a contest, offering  a discount or just writing informative  content.  

**6\. Promote to your list\!** I find that  conversions are highest when I promote  to my list. If you’ve done any online  marketing then you’ve probably heard 

ten   
*www\.thevamagazine.com* 

the phrase “The Money is in the List”and  it’s absolutely true. It’s not uncommon  for your customers to not buy from you  the first time they land on your site. Most  people think about things and have to have  contact with you several times before they  make the decision to buy. By building a  list of targeted prospects you’re greatly  improving your chances of affiliate sales. 

Once you have a database of subscribers then  your list will be like gold to you. Imagine  power of sending one email with a product  promotion to a few hundred or thousand people  and then watching your sales come in. This is  the power of a targeted list of subscribers. 

However, it’s not just about building a list;  you also need to take the time to establish a  relationship with that list. If you never email  your list and out of the blue send them a  promotion email then the chances are you’ll have  more “unsubscribes” from your list than sales. 

Building a good relationship and rapport with  your list will take a little time but it is worth  it once you get the balance right. Try sending  an email at least once a week (any longer and  they may forget who you are) with content  that balances providing information and  promotions and you don’t need to promote  a product with every mailing. Give your  list members value: valuable tips, valuable  information, case studies, and other tips to help  them. Once your list members grow to trust  you they will eagerly purchase items through  you because they feel like you have taken the  time to establish a relationship with them. 

Now you know why you should build a list but  how do you start building it? No matter which  way you go about it, having a list of targeted  customers is a great way to build your affiliate  marketing business. 

1\. Create a report, e-course or eBook to give  away free in return for signing up to the  list. Make your offer impossible to resist.  

*about the author*   
*Marketing* 

Remember you want them to sign up so  give away something of benefit. 

2\. Create a separate landing page for your  newsletter subscribers. In a time of  increasing SPAM, it’s getting harder and  harder to get people to give you their  email addresses. Even if you’re giving  something away for free, you’ll still need  to sell that “free gift”. Build a simple opt  in page which clearly explains the benefits  of subscribing to your list. 

3\. Write and distribute articles to the online  article directories and in your author bio  include a link back to your newsletter page.  If you write good quality articles then you’re  increasing the chances that the reader will  want to click over and learn more. 

**And finally here are a few tips to help  you avoid those dreaded mistakes many  beginners make in affiliate marketing.** 

**Banner Central –** Banners don’t often work  as well as text links. Of course, it does depend  on your subject and you should test both  individually to determine what works best but  you’re often better off sticking to text links  rather than turning your website into banner  central. 

**Unrelated Products –** You’d be surprised at  the number of people who do this. Make sure  all offers on your website are related to the  context on your site \- if you have a site about  being a VA, don’t offer pet care products. You  might be tempted to promote those products  because you are a dog lover but stick to what  your target market is looking for. 

**Missing your Target Market –** Before starting  any business venture (including affiliate  marketing) you should always know who your  target market is. To become a super affiliate  you’ll need to build a website which speaks  directly to the market you are trying to attract  and you’ll need to understand the needs of that  specific market.  

**Hype, Hype, Hype –** Avoid it all costs. People  can smell hype a mile away and will run away  quick with their credit card clutched tightly  in their hands. If you review products on your  website make sure those reviews are honest.  More importantly, try to take the time to  actually use the product or at least look into it  then you can genuinely speak about its features  and benefits. Don’t forget to add the not so  good sides of the product too. If the product  is lacking in some way, say so. People will  appreciate your honesty and grow to trust you. 

**Not Tracking Sales –** In order to know whether  an affiliate programme is worth promoting you  need to track how well it converts. Try out  different things in your promotions and track  each one of them.

**Nine Management  Lessons from the  London 2012 Games** 

WRITER   
Kathleen Drum

**Reflecting on the process of putting together the greatest show in the  world has revealed some very useful business insights.** 

**![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()**twelve *www\.thevamagazine.com* 

Three months after the closing ceremony of  the London 2012 Paralympic Games and the  70,000 volunteers are well and truly back in  the routine of their daily lives. Many have  written about their experiences being part of  the Games, but few have considered the impact  of that experience on their professional lives. 

Kathleen Drum, a volunteer performer in  the ‘Pandemonium’ section of the Olympic  Games Opening Ceremony, lists the top nine  management lessons that can be applied in the  working environment: 

1\. There is only one Leader 

The Opening Ceremony was the creative vision  of Danny Boyle, and we were all there to help  him turn that vision into a reality. We had  dance captains, choreographers and producers  to help us along the way, but at the end of the  day what Danny said, we did. 

2\. Create trust within the team 

Against advice from LOCOG, Danny showed  the volunteers the model of the ‘green and  pleasant land’ opening sequence of the show,  and explained how that section would run  and where we would come into it. He asked  us not to use social media or give away the  secret. Later on, during the final rehearsals, the  Twitter hash-tag \#save the surprise (started by  a volunteer in response to leaked information)  was adopted. Danny asked the audience at  both rehearsals to ‘save the surprise’ and, in the  majority, they did. By trusting people with a  secret he created an atmosphere where no-one  wanted to let him down. 

3\. Be clear on the vision 

At our first rehearsal we were shown a short  video of what Danny wanted to achieve for the  Industrial Revolution section; it included CGI  images of people marching and performing in  time, chimneys rising from the ground, lots of  grime and smoke and, surprisingly, the New  Zealand All Blacks doing the haka before a  rugby match. We were asked to think in terms  of the strength and unison of the All Blacks  team and their power when performing the  haka. This served as a good reference point for  our performance on the day. 

*about the author*   
*leadership* 

4\. Make sure everyone knows their place on  the team 

We spent a lot of time getting to know our  place in the crowd – our ‘home base’. We played  a number of games running from one section to  the next and returning to home base. We were  encouraged to learn the names of those people  in front, behind and to each side of our place  to help orient us. When we were divided into  ‘counties’ later in the rehearsal process, this  continued – we had ‘buddy lines’ and learnt the  roles associated with that place. 

5\. Use a common language 

The great majority of the performers were not  from theatre backgrounds so a lot of terms of  reference were new. Our producer, Steve, would  explain what they meant and we were expected  to use the term and respond correctly – ‘field  of play (the stage)’ ‘IEM (in ear monitor)’ and  ‘vom’ (an exit from the field of play) soon  become terms were we comfortable with. It was  a useful reminder that speaking in acronyms  and industry terms can alienate team members  when used without explanation. 

6\. Communicate the plan 

There were 15 weeks and approximately 150  hours of rehearsals, and we knew the dates  (and most of the timings and locations) of the  majority of these by the middle of January.  That gave us six months to make our plans,  arrange days off from work, and generally  commit to the time required mentally, as well  as in our calendars. Planning ahead meant that  a seemingly impossible logistical task was able  to go to plan. 

7\. Set expectations 

At every rehearsal we were told the plan for  the day, what we needed to achieve by the  end, when our breaks would be and what time  we would finish. We felt comfortable with  the expectations that were set and a sense of  achievement when they were met. 

8\. Reward commitment 

We were asked to attend an additional rehearsal  on the four-day Jubilee weekend, on the day of  the Thames Pageant. This request came quite  late in the proceedings when a number of the  

team had already made plans. Those people  who attended that rehearsal were presented  with London 2012 ‘ceremonies’ t-shirts. It  was a great incentive, marked us all as ‘special’  and we wore them with pride at the following  rehearsals. The rest of the team did not receive  their t-shirts until a number of weeks later. 

9\. If things aren’t working – fix them The first time we arrived for a rehearsal at the  stadium, we were told that the sets of stairs that  exited the field of play were too narrow. The  production team had arrived before us, realised  there was an issue and immediately arranged for  the carpenters to rebuild the stairs: one set had  already been fixed by the time we arrived and  the rest were replaced before our next rehearsal. 10\. Don’t be afraid of change\!  

Even with the end goal in sight, you can still  adjust course to ensure you achieve it. At every  rehearsal we received updated instructions  on when to do our choreographed moves,  how many sets to do and at what times. The  instructions changed right up to, and during the  Opening Ceremony itself. This final ‘tweaking’  made a better experience for the audience and  for us. Because our trust in the leadership and  its decision-making had been established early,  we felt confident in our ability to deliver on the  requested changes, and knew that we would be  appreciated for our efforts. 

There aren’t many projects that would create  the same logistical challenges (not to mention  people management challenges) of the Opening  Ceremony. But the lessons from this amazing  event could definitely improve teamwork and  project management, even in the smallest  company.

Kathleen Drum has spent the last six years working on the London 2012 Olympic Games as the on-site PA and Office  Manager for UK Power Networks – and also found the time to perform in the Opening Ceremony. Born in New  Zealand, Kathleen has lived in London for over 15 years and has worked in a variety of industries including tourism,  finance and recruitment. Kathleen is an advocate of self-development and life-long learning, and is the current vice 

President of the New Zealand Society UK and a member of EUMA (European Management Assistants), a not-for-profit  association for PAs.

*Business Strategy* 

WRITER   
Sharon   
Williams 

**Does Your Vision  Keep You Fired Up?** 

When is the last time you had a dream?

When you launched your business, did you let  

your mind wander or allow your imagination to  

run wild? Did your brain explode with powerful  

thoughts, culminating in an unbounded  

enthusiasm to start your day and drive your  

business forward? 

Developing a strategic vision statement can  

serve as the roadmap that propels your business  

forward 5, 10, 15 years. It represents what  

is most important to you and what you fully  

commit to. It inspires you\! 

A strategic vision is the roadmap of the  

company’s future – the direction it is headed,  

the customer focus it should have, the market  

position it will occupy, business activities to  

be pursued and the capabilities you plan to  

develop. It outlines long-term direction of the  

business and provides a sense of purposeful  

action in everything you do. Ultimately, it  

describes your company’s destination and how  

you will get there. 

Sometimes it is difficult to write a strategic  

vision statement: new entrepreneurs aren’t  

aware they should have one and experienced  

business owners are so caught up in  

running their business that they may have  

forgotten it. 

FOURTEEN *www\.thevamagazine.com* 

**Bold, Concise, Forethought** 

A strategic vision must be bold enough to  inspire you. It is the dream which covers all  aspects of your business as you look forward. It  must be a concise summary of all your thoughts.  For example, you may want to be considered as  the go to person in your niche market. Another  example may be that you want to expand your  range of services to provide a complete turnkey  operation. Keep in mind that your vision is the  foundation to your true success. 

The following are characteristics of a good  strategic vision statement: 

•  The Strategic Vision Statement provides  a picture of what your business will look  like in 5 to 10 years from now.  

•  It represents a dream that is beyond what  you think possible. Visioning takes you  out beyond the present. 

•  The Strategic Vision Statement creates a  vivid image in your head that provokes  emotion and excitement. It poses a  challenge that inspires and engages you  and others you are associated with.  

Some of the benefits of creating a Strategic  Vision Statement include: 

•  It empowers you and focuses your efforts •  It forces energy for greater effectiveness •  It raises the standard of excellence •  It establishes meaning for today •  It gives hope for the future 

•  It brings unity to community 

•  It provides a sense of continuity •  It raises commitment level 

•  It brings positive change 

**Activity** 

Steps to take to achieve your strategic vision: 

**A.  Be Specific –** goals should be  straightforward and emphasise what you  want to happen. Specifics help us to focus  our efforts and clearly define what we are  going to do. Specific is the What, Why,  and How. 

*about the author*   
*Business Strategy* 

**B.  Be Measurable –** If you can’t measure it,  you can’t manage it. In the broadest sense,  the whole goal is a measurement for the  final outcome. Usually, there are several  short-term or small measurements that  can be built into the long term vision. 

**C.  Establish Concrete Criteria –** Measure  progress towards attaining your goals and  accomplishing your ultimate vision. 

**D.  Be Attainable –** When you identify  goals that are most important to you, you  begin to figure out ways you can make  them come true. You develop attitudes,  abilities, skills and financial capacity to  reach them.  

**E.  Be Reachable –** This is not a synonym  for “easy”. Realistic in this case means  do-able. It means the learning curve is  not a vertical slope; the skills needed are  available or attainable; and you are willing  to break through barriers. Be sure to set  goals that you can attain with some effort\!  Too difficult and you set the stage for  failure, but too low sends the message that  you aren’t very capable. Set the bar high  enough for a satisfying achievement. 

**F.  Be Timely –** Set the 5-10 year timeline  and then break that schedule down into  year-to-year segments, month to month  and week to week targets. If you don’t  set a time, the commitment is too vague.  Without a timeframe, there’s no sense of  urgency to start taking action now.  

**Complete This Exercise** 

Here’s an exercise I’d like you to do: visualise  walking into a room three years from now and  shaking hands with yourself. Who are you?  What is your life like? What is your business  like? Write down what you saw. Don’t let  yourself wake up in three years and say, “I’m  three years older and I just happened to get  here.” Clarify your vision so that you can grow  into it. 

In a nutshell, keep in mind that your past  shaped you to be who you are. It will help you  build a solid foundation and avoid making  common mistakes. Be clear and concise so as  to not seem confusing. Your strategic vision  statement should be concise and to the point.  Always present a positive image as it impacts  how you are perceived by others. Ensure that  your vision inspires, as it will help you to  maintain your momentum. 

**Be Bold and Take Charge** 

Remember, be bold\! Take total charge of your  vision. Let the world know that you mean  business and that you are going places. Don’t  be afraid to use your imagination and showcase  your level of skill. It shows you care about  the way your company is perceived. Potential  customers may feel that you will do the same  for them. 

Visualise the end product and its impact. This  is important to the development of the vision  statement as it sets a course for the vision. Take  time to think things through from beginning  to end. This will help you understand where  you are going. You do not have to know all the  steps at this point. However, you do need to  know the direction in which you want to grow  your business. 

It is sometimes difficult to write a vision  statement that truly encapsulates your vision  for your company. When you write your vision  statement, make sure that you have chosen the  vision that is most important to you. If you  don’t fully believe in your vision statement, you  won’t be able to fully commit to it and writing  a vision statement that you can’t or won’t fully  commit to is a waste of time.

*Communication*   
**Write Successful  And Persuasive  Communications** 

WRITER   
Heather Baker 

Do you want to ensure people read your messages? Do you want to convince  people to do what you want? Some emails we can’t wait to read and we are  excited by their content. Others, however, we start to read and immediately  delete, or don’t even open at all. What is it that makes the difference?

you as a professional with high standards and  

this can impact on the general impression that  

colleagues and clients have of you. As well as  

improving your own career prospects, it can  

also improve the success of your business.  

Clients will often select a supplier based on  

the look of their correspondence and the ease  

of reading their documents or the tone of their  

emails. 

There can be so many barriers to good written  

communication: incorrect grammar, spelling  

and punctuation; not considering your reader;  

misjudging relationships; not understanding  

the issue; tight deadlines; using an inappropriate  

tone or unsuitable words; being too emotional  

and lack of preparation. 

Preparation is vital before sending any business  

communication. This may only take a few  

seconds for a simple email but *always stop before*  

*you send.* 

The first thing you must do is establish the  

purpose of your communication; it is easy to  

say the objective is “to obtain information”  

or “to arrange a meeting” but if you keep  

asking yourself “why?” you will usually come  

to the answer that it is for the success of the  

organisation. Every communication you send  

in business can impact on your company’s  

performance. 

First of all, let’s consider why writing skills are  so important in business. It is essential to be  able to communicate your message precisely  and concisely to avoid misunderstandings  and wasting time. The majority of business  communication is now written and there are  many examples of misinterpretations.   
When we write to somebody we are establishing  a relationship with that person. It is very  easy, because of the lack of visual and vocal  communication, to cause misunderstandings or  even offence.  

Conveying your ideas precisely and laying out  your documents effectively gives an image of  

This doesn’t just apply to emails, it is relevant  to letters, reports, minutes, tenders, proposals,  brochures and websites and social media. 

Once you’ve established your purpose, the  next part of your preparation is to think  about the people who will be reading your  correspondence. The more you know about  

SIXTEEN *www\.thevamagazine.com* 

your reader(s), the better you can tailor your  correspondence to make it more effective. It  is always more difficult to tailor a message to  more than one person. 

What is their age, knowledge, language,  culture, background, gender? How busy are  they, how do they like to receive information,  how important is your message to them? What  is their personality type, where do they come  in the organisation’s hierarchy, what is your  relationship with them? Are there any special  needs to be considered? These are just some of  the factors which may influence what and how  you write. 

To continue your preparation, ensure you  have all the information you need, anticipate  your reader’s questions and provide answers  in advance. Ensure you have a clear structure,  particularly if your correspondence is complex.  For emails, give your message an informative  subject so your reader can easily prioritise. 

When you have composed your correspondence,  it is vital to proofread before you send.  Preferably leave the communication for a short  while and go back it with ‘fresh eyes’. Don’t  forget you are not just checking for spelling  and punctuation, you are also checking for the  tone, the content and whether it is written  fittingly for the reader.  

In most circumstances in business writing  we are trying to persuade somebody to do  something for us – give information, attend  a meeting, send us money. It is, therefore,  important to use techniques that will make  your messages more persuasive. Here are some  ways you could achieve this: 

•  Continually maintaining a high standard  in your business writing is the basic rule of  persuasive writing. If your correspondence  looks unprofessional, people will not want  to help you. 

•  Many people still write in a style more  suited to the 1950s than the 2010s with  phrases like ‘If you have any queries, please  do not hesitate to contact me’ or ‘please be  informed that the documents you require  will be despatched in due course’. These  

*about the author*   
*Communication* 

sorts of phrases are known as business  clichés and they are to be avoided. It is  lazy, unimaginative and boring to rely  on hackneyed business phrases. I am not  suggesting we should use text-speak  but nowadays a natural style is much  more effective, whilst maintaining an  appropriate formality or informality. 

•  What’s In It For Me (WIIFM): Tell your  reader why it is to their advantage to do  what you want them to do. 

When a Sales Manager wanted his reps to give  him their monthly figures so he could write a  report for his Director, they were all ‘too busy’.  When he reframed the question and asked for  the figures so he could calculate their bonuses,  you can imagine the very different response\! 

•  Offer solutions rather than problems. This  is particularly effective if you are trying  to persuade senior management. They  want their staff to assist them, not give  them more problems. If you offer viable  solutions you may also get the resolution  you prefer. 

•  When we have to ask people something  which we expect they won’t like, we tend  to write in a manner that anticipates  their dissatisfaction. Our style becomes  very apologetic and negative, which can  actually exacerbate their displeasure.  Where possible, try to turn bad news into  good news. For example: 

*‘We are sorry to tell you that the information you  need will not be available for at least two hours. We  apologise for the inconvenience this may cause you.’*  (cliché alert\!). 

Perhaps we could have written: 

*‘We are pleased to confirm that the information you  need will be available within three hours and, if we  can get it to you sooner, we will do.’* 

This is called ‘reframing’ and is generally a  good principle. It will ensure a better reaction  from your reader. 

•  Use appropriate vocabulary. People build  rapport initially through non-verbal  

signals and tone of voice, but we also build  rapport through the language we use.  Obviously if two people speak the same  language they have a rapport but we can  take this a couple of steps further. 

In the evening, do you have ‘dinner’, ‘tea’  or ‘supper’? Do you have a ‘lounge’, ‘living  room’, ‘sitting room’, ‘front room’, ‘parlour’  or ‘drawing room’? All these words give away  a great deal about our background and we  automatically ‘warm’ to people who use the  same words as we do. How much more effective  then would it be to use the same words as your  reader? How much more persuasive could you  be? If you have good rapport with someone,  they will go out of their way to help you. 

We can take this a step further, Neuro Linguistic Programming (NLP) teaches  about representational systems. We gather  information through our senses and we all  have preferences in the way we use these. Our  preferences can be reflected in the vocabulary  we use so, for example, a visual person may  say ‘I see what you mean’ whereas an auditory  person could say ‘that sounds interesting’. By  using similar words in correspondence, to those  used by your reader, you can build rapport and  be more persuasive. 

Try and avoid negative words like ‘just’ (‘I just  work for the MD’) and, where possible when  giving feedback, replace ‘but’ with ‘and’.If  you say to someone ‘you did a very good job  organising the conference but there were  problems with the car parking’, the recipient  may well feel rather deflated; everything  before the ‘but’ is forgotten. Try rephrasing  and say ‘you did a very good job organising  the conference and, when we sort out the car  parking problems next year, it will be a terrific  event’. 

So, what is it that makes the difference? The five  Ps – preparation, purpose, people, proofreading  and persuasion. If what you’re doing isn’t  working, do something else – choose to excel at  business writing. 

Heather had over 20 years’ experience as a secretary and PA before setting up Baker Thompson Associates Limited in  2000 *(www\.bakerthompsonassoc.co.uk).* The company specialises in the training and development of PAs and administrative  staff. She travels around the world working with large and small organisations to enable their office staff and PAs to  work more effectively. As well as PA/admin workshops, Heather facilitates sessions in business writing, proofreading,  minute taking and much more. 

Heather is the author of *Successful Minute Taking ; Meeting the Challenge* and *Successful Business Writing,* in which  you learn even more about business writing. She is the creator of the BakerWrite speedwriting system and  the author of *Speedwriting*. As well as tutor-led courses, BakerWrite speedwriting can also be learnt online at  *www\.BakerWrite.com.* 

Heather presented at the Office\* 2012 show in September.

**Profile – Diana Ennen** 

 Diana Ennen has been a leader in the Virtual Assistant industry since opening  her business in 1985\. She’s the president of Virtual Word Publishing *http\://www\. virtualwordpublishing.com,* where she specialises in publicity and marketing and  PR and VA Coaching. She’s the author of numerous books on business start ups including *Virtual Assistant – The Series: Become a Highly Successful, Sought  After VA* (with Kelly Poelker) and *So You Want to be a Work-at-Home Mom: A  Christians’ Guide to Starting a Home-Based Business.* She has been featured on  CBS4News, Fox News, CNN, USA Today, Wall Street Journal.

**Can we start with a little background information? Where are you  from?** 

I’m from sunny Florida. I’ve lived here for over 30 years with my husband  (Greg) and three kids (Jeremy, Amanda, and Amber.)  

**What is your background?** 

I have been in business since 1985\. I specialise in PR and marketing now  but over the years I have done pretty much everything\! Before owning  my own business I was a medical and legal secretary. In the early days of  my business I did a lot of legal transcription so I did get my Paralegal  degree. Once I started selling my books I decided I loved the marketing  aspect of it and that’s when I focused on PR and marketing. 

**You are very active as an author both in the VA and the  Christian arenas. How did you get involved in this?** 

I was very fortunate and worked with Jill Hart of  

Christian Work at Home Moms. We did radio  

shows together, wrote articles together and so much  

more. We loved working together for both the  

work-at-home moms and then Christian women  

as well. Then one day she asked me if I wanted to  

write a book with her. I was delighted to as I love  

co-authoring. It was so great writing it as we got  to include some spiritual principles in the book. Then  one of the top Christian publishers was interested in it and  they published it. One of the joys of doing the book was our TV  segment with Homekeepers (one of the leading Christian TV networks).  You can see our interview on my website *http\://www\.virtualwordpublishing. com/media.htm.* 

**You also coach Virtual Assistants. What is the difference between  training and coaching?** 

I love to coach Virtual Assistants. I think the main difference between  training and coaching is that I work one-to-one with clients, walking  them through the steps of starting and operating their business. Training  is more structured and topic driven but both are important. 

**You have been in business for 24 years this year. What are the main  changes you have seen in the time you have been in business?**   
Without doubt it’s the changes in technology. When I started computers  were the big thing but now it’s all the latest toys and I have a hard  time keeping up\! I believe I’m still the only person on the planet with a  Blackberry just because I love it. 

Social media has been another big change \- to be able to connect with  others on Facebook and Twitter rocks. The personal connection with  other Virtual Assistants and business owners allows me to not only grow  my business but my skills as well. 

**What inspires and motivates you?** 

My VA friends and helping others be successful in their business.  

**PR seems to be a real passion of yours. What are your top  tips for a VA wanting to do some PR for their business?** 

1)  Be clear on what you can do and what you can  accomplish. You often need to educate the clients  

on what can be done in the time provided. 

2)  Be the best. That includes having the best  

resources. I pay for services like Profnet and  

Cision.com so that I do have the best databases  

and resources for my clients. 

3)  Continue to learn. I attend a lot of classes so that I  am constantly growing. 

4)  Get the most out of your PR efforts by combining several  things together to get the most impact. So in a media kit  I will write a press release that can be sent out as well as  added to a pitch. The articles I write will contribute to my  social media by forming the basis of Tweets and go into blog  posts, newsletters, etc. When you combine your PR and go  full circle with all you do you get more results. 

**What has been the highlight of your career so far and why?** 

It has to be writing and marketing my books with Kelly Poelker and  Jill Hart. Kelly and I have become such good friends over the years and  it’s fun to have someone to share things with. We’ve accomplished so  much including getting our books in many colleges today and that’s just  thrilling. 

eighteen *www\.thevamagazine.com*   
*Profile – Diana Ennen Profile – Diana Ennen* 

**What are the main challenges facing the Virtual Assistant  profession at the moment?** 

Fortunately, it’s no longer telling others what a VA is. I would say  helping Virtual Assistants charge what they are worth. Too many aren’t  these days. 

**What advice would you give someone just starting in the role?** 

To really know what you are doing and learn as much as you can. Decide  what you want to do and make sure that you have the skills and passion  to do it. Then just get out there and do it.

**So what’s next for Diana Ennen? Where do you want to be in 5  years time?** 

Lots of exciting things\! I hope to continue coaching and training and  offering more PR classes but I absolutely love my clients and know I will  always continue working with them. I totally love providing PR services. 

I also hope to do more training for Virtual Assistant Multi-VA Teams  as well. I’d love to be able to work with different teams and train their  subcontractors and team members on how to do PR. 

nineteen  
*Marketing* 

**How to get higher open  rates for your email  campaigns** 

WRITER 

Tamara Baranova 

Are your email open rates letting you down? You have such a brilliant offer,  fantastic product or a superb business but your emails are not getting opened.  How do you get the message across to your prospective customers or clients in  the first instance so they open your mail and discover your product or service?  In the same way as you assess your incoming emails. 

that is open to communicating and forming a  

relationship with them. 

**‘Subject’ line** 

Subscribers consider many things before they  

open an email. How busy they are, what they  

expected when they subscribed or their past  

interactions with you. There isn’t a magic  

formula but a compelling subject line can  

persuade them to read further. The following  

strategies may help you get a better open rate  

as, according to Marketing Sherpa’s 2010  

Email Marketing Benchmark Report, the three  

most compelling subject lines are: 

•  A discount offer – people sign up to  

mailing lists to obtain special deals so if  

your subject line contains information  

about a deal you can expect a higher  

response rate. 

•  A free product offer – use the subject line  

to tell subscribers about your offer. 

**So, what makes you open an email?** 

•  It’s something you’re expecting? •  You are a fan or follower of the sender and  want to see what’s new? 

•  The mail preview is entertaining,  surprising/shocking or inspiring? 

To increase your email open rates you need to  think about the “From” and “Subject” lines,  Snippets and Preview Panes. 

**‘From’ line** 

This is probably the first thing that your  subscriber will see so get this right and your  email will be opened. Remember that the  amount of text displayed differs so you want to  keep it at 20-32 characters – iPhone mail only  displays 20 characters\! So make it short, sharp  

and to the point. Make the contact personal  – use a person’s name, especially if you have  a representative that is easily recognised by  your subscribers. Don’t forget the name of your  company will be instantly recognisable to your  list and never use a @gmail, @hotmail or @ 

yahoo account. 

Your email should be a two-way conversation  with your prospective customers and clients  so using “Do Not Reply” will deter people  opening the mail and stop the conversation. It is  regarded as offensive and sends out the message  that you do not want to communicate except  to get your message across. Your company  will benefit by building relationships with  your subscribers as people want to be heard.  If you are not open to listening you may well  lose that prospect when they turn to someone    
•  A familiar brand name – a legitimate  association between you and a recognised  brand can increase the open rate. 

People like to know that others have used your  product or service and are pleased with the  results. Naming people and how they have  benefited from your product, helps subscribers  trust you. 

**Subject line best practice** 

•  Be clear and straightforward with your  offer, use words that people understand. •  Be helpful – your list exists because  subscribers believe you can help them. •  Telling them that you have the ‘how’ gives  them a good reason to open your email. •  Ask a question because this appeals to a  reader’s curiosity. Consider the content of 

twenty   
*www\.thevamagazine.com* 

your email and what questions it answers  and create a question as your subject line. •  You can create urgency in any part of your  email but it is most effective in the subject  line as if they don’t open the email they  won’t see the deadline anywhere else. •  An expiry date on offers and discounts  persuades subscribers to open before the  deal is no longer available. 

By split testing your email campaign with  different clients you can see which email  gets opened more. Do this by sending out  two different versions of the same message  but with different subject lines. Analyse  the number of opens you get for each of the  subject lines and this will give you a better  understanding of your audience and what they  respond to. 

**Snippets** 

Some email programs display a preview of the  text at the top of your email. This snippet could  provide an added incentive for subscribers to  open your email. So choose the primary text  carefully and again split test to determine  which words are more successful. 

Try moving the ‘unsubscribe’ or ‘whitelist’  request from the top of your email to the top  of the sidebar. They are still easily accessed but  you can add more compelling text. 

Programmes in which the snippet pane will  show in are: 

•  Gmail, 

•  Outlook, 

•  iPhone, 

•  iPad. 

**Getting Snippets to work in your campaign** 

If you have a logo, image or header at the top  these can be included in your snippet by setting  to ‘alt’ text so if the images don’t display you  will have text showing instead. Another tweak  which will make the snippet disappear is to  assign ‘alt’ text to a tiny image, then make it  invisible by matching it to your background  

*about the author*   
*Marketing* 

colour. These both work in Gmail and Outlook.  As always, split test to see which option gets a  bigger response. 

Be aware that different browsers will show  different amounts of your email in the snippet  area. A high percentage of your readers will be  reading your email in Gmail or Outlook or on  iPhones or iPads, so consider what amount of  your text will appear. 

Remember that what you have at the top  of your email appears in the snippet so use  keywords here. If it’s at the top of your email  a pre-header, a header or the ‘alt’ text from an  image will be in your snippet. 

Screen size will affect the amount of text shown,  so remove any less urgent items to the sidebar. 

It can take a little practice to write snippets  that smoothly connect your subject line with  your header but it’s worth trying and making  contact with more of your subscribers. 

**Preview Panes** 

When you send business to consumer (B2C)  email marketing campaigns 53% of subscribers  see your preview pane but with business to  business (B2B) the percentage is closer to 80%.  The preview pane lets users look at part of the  email before deciding whether to open it. With  some easy design changes you can encourage  subscribers to open your email. 

**The space you have to work with varies  between email clients** 

Yahoo shows a horizontal strip of the email  but others including Outlook display a vertical  column. To take advantage of this space (both  horizontal and vertical) fill the top left of the  email with items that grab attention. Including  your logo, company name or any other  recognisable branding that will strengthen a  weak subject line by providing a known and  trusted name. 

Use the top space for a strong deal or for a table  of contents so that what’s in your email can be  seen at a glance. Time is of the essence \- some  

may not have time to read all of your email \-  but your call to action might be just what they  are looking for so placing this in the preview  pane can lead to action. 

Depending on your design it may be difficult  to fit all this into that small space, cramming  too much into a small space can be off-putting.  Inserting a side column allows two items to  exist near the top in harmony. 

**Make the Preview Pane compelling to open  your campaign** 

Designing the preview panes so they work  well may mean removing things that are less  compelling. Large images and headers need  assessing as to their efficiency. 

Consider: 

•  When images are turned off they will  appear as a blank space. 

•  If they are too large they will take forever  to load. 

•  Do they create a need to find out more? 

If they don’t then more compelling content  can replace them. Links that mirror the tabs  on your website can be helpful for click backs  to your site. They can get in the way of more  important items so consider shrinking or  reducing the amount you use. Better still, they  could be displayed across the bottom of your  email. 

Ads – place these away from the preview pane  (unless you want subscribers to click through  to advertisers).  

**Wondering whether it’s all worth the  trouble?**  

Split-test your broadcast in your email  marketing provider to find the answer and the  most effective way to contact your subscribers.  If you see that your results (opens, clicks,  social sharing, comments and interactions) are  steadily growing from campaign to campaign –  you are on the right track, well done\!

twenty one   
*Niche Focus* 

**Speakers and Coaches  – A Perfect Niche for  Virtual Assistants** 

WRITER   
Kelly   
Poelker 

Ask a Virtual Assistant to name their greatest challenge and they will say it’s  finding their niche. 

It’s difficult.  

First they had to identify their skills and  talents and match those with their interests and  experiences. Then they had to find the market  that would allow them to best utilise those  talents in an area that interests them. Many  struggle to do this so they take the easy road  by targeting anyone and everyone. They think  the more they target the better their chances  of landing clients. Unfortunately that’s exactly  what you don’t want to do. 

So what exactly is a niche?  

Easy, a niche is a target market or industry  whose needs fit well with a particular skill  set. The great thing about the VA industry is  that the opportunities for matching the right  talents with the right niche are tremendous.  Of the many niches you can target (including  estate agents, authors, small businesses) the one  that we have found works perfectly and is one  of our favourites, is working with speakers and  coaches. That is the focus of our business and  why we know it works. So let’s go into exactly  what that looks like. 

Speakers and coaches have very similar roles  in their businesses and that is why you often  see VAs target them both. More often than  not, coaches are also speakers and vice versa.  From routine tasks such as email and calendar  maintenance to higher-level support like  marketing and booking speaking engagements,  

*about the author* 

the versatile VA can handle a multitude of  support tasks for these clients. 

This match of VA and ideal client can prove to  be very rewarding for both parties. It doesn’t  take the client long to realise that they can  make more money and be more successful when  they get the assistance they need and not try to  do everything themselves. The VA can tap into  their broad skillset and find variety in their  everyday tasks. 

Here are several services that can be offered to  speakers and coaches: 

•  PR and marketing – article and press  release marketing, building a brand,  promoting events, pitching the media,  list building 

•  Teleseminars – scheduling, promoting,  recording, transcribing 

•  Book Speaking Events – research  prospective venues, create pitches, lead  follow up, send out speaker packets 

•  Shopping carts – maintenance, product  additions, auto responders, broadcasts,  affiliate programs 

•  Websites – design and maintenance, blog  postings, membership sites, keyword  research 

•  Document creation – develop courses,  homework assignments, e-books,  presentations 

•  SEO/web analysis – websites, blogs,  videos, social media, sales pages, keyword  research   
•  Social networking – enhance social media  presence on Facebook, Twitter, LinkedIn  groups 

•  Research – target audience demographics,  information for teleseminars, statistics,  new places to market 

•  Brainstorming ideas – products, markets,  alliances, JV partners 

Now that might just seem like a shopping list  of services but think about it this way. 

You have a speaker who routinely speaks at large  conferences or offers teleseminars. They also  have a thriving practice with regular clients.  You, as their virtual assistant, are able to step  in and handle all the administrative tasks that  then allows the client to speak more, coach  more, market more—you get the picture. You  can also be instrumental in managing travel for  their speaking engagements, coordinating the  contract process, gathering post-event reviews,  providing customer service…  

Can you see what a valuable asset to their team  a VA can be? 

So take the time to learn about the speaking  industry to determine if working with speakers  and coaches is a good fit for your VA business.  You might want to link up with those who  support speakers on social media and gain from  the tips they share on Twitter and Facebook.

Connect with them online at: @kellypoelker @dianaennen and facebook.com/becomeava
