*Finance*   
**10 Tips for Cashflow**    
**Management** 

‘Turnover is vanity, profit is sanity, cash is king’. Do you take  this saying to heart in your business? Having enough cash in  the business is critical for both your sanity and your success. 

WRITER   
Antonia Chitty 

In the current economic climate, many  businesses are struggling and this can affect a  VA business too, as clients make cut backs. You  can improve your business finances by learning  some smart ways to manage your money. Here  are ten tips to help your business cash flow:  

1\. Stay on top of invoicing and send out  bills as quickly as you can so you get your  income as soon as possible. Make sure that  you have a system for invoicing, whether  you set up a database of client details in  Excel or use an accounts programme like  Sage, QuickBooks or FreeAgent. Think  about the terms for payment that you  offer each client: do you need your bills  paid on receipt of invoice, within 14 days  or 30 days? If you have completed work  it can be reasonable to ask for payment  on receipt. If you will incur up-front costs  on a project you may want to ask for a  deposit.  

2\. Pay staff and freelancers promptly. Staff  will rely on income from you, so be a great  boss and pay on time at the end of each  week or month. If you commission work  out to freelancers and want to develop a  good working relationship, so you can go  back to them time after time, pay them  promptly too\! 

3\. Pay other bills only when they are due.  Check each invoice for payment terms and  pay on the last day possible for the money  to get to the payee in time. This ensures  

*about the author*   
that the money stays in your bank account  for as long as possible, helping you to  cover your costs while waiting for clients  to pay you.  

4\. Deposit cheques promptly. This all helps  ensure that there is as much money as  possible IN your business account, rather  than sitting on your desk\! 

5\. If you don’t have one already, look for an  interest-paying business deposit account  for your business reserve and sweep money  into this account regularly. Even though  interest rates are low, a few per cent  interest is better than nothing. By doing  this you will get even more benefit from  invoicing promptly and paying in cheques  as soon as you get them.  

6\. Check your bank charges and shop around  for a better deal. You could save every  month if you switch or you may find your  existing bank improves their offer when  you explain to them you want to move to  cut costs.  

7\. Change the way you bill clients. If  you have regular clients who want the  same sort of work every month, set up  subscription programmes. This works  especially well for VAs who offer social  media services. If clients pay by direct  debit your business then has a reliable  income that you don’t have to chase each  month. Incentivise clients by offering a  small discount or bonus service. You’ll  save time on invoicing too.  

8\. Look for additional income sources. If you  always recommend a particular product or  programme to your clients, see if there is  an affiliate scheme. If you review business  books on your blog, embed an Amazon  affiliate link within the title or image. If  you have a popular website, allow a few  relevant adverts. All these income streams  can add up and improve your cash flow.  

9\. At times you may need to invest to grow  your business. If you are expanding you  may need an overdraft to allow you to pay  bills. It can be hard to get credit if your  business is in difficulties, so ask your bank  manager about credit terms well before  you need it. Check around for competitive  rates.  

10\. Chase debts firmly. Ensure that one  person is responsible for this and that they  allocate time every week. Have a clear set  of actions to follow from a first reminder  to sending in debt collectors if necessary.  By setting up the steps in advance it is  easier to put them in to practice when you  need. 

Follow these ten steps and keep track of the  money coming in and out of your business.  You will soon find that you are on the way to  having a healthy amount in your business bank  account.  

Antonia Chitty helps business owners succeed. She provides practical training and helpful tips at *www\.aceinspire.com.*  Her latest books include Blogging: *The Essential Guide (Need2Know)* and *Making Money Online (Hale Books).*

*www\.thevamagazine.com* twenty three   
*The Events Masterclass* 

WRITER 

Lorne   
**An alternative to  PowerPoint** 

Armstrong 

Yes PowerPoint has its uses for presentations in events. But they are limited.

![]()  
If you are a nervous speaker or presenter then  you might use PowerPoint as a crutch to get  you through your content. If you want to  ruin a *live* presentation to communicate a pile  of factual information then use lots of slides,  charts and bullets in PowerPoint. If you have  a lot more information to deliver than you can  edit down to a few simple messages then use a  deck of PowerPoint slides to make you feel like  you have done your job. If you like to involve  your audience through submission or if you  think that talking at people is actually going  to have any impact on what they do then go for  it…PowerPoint will be your friend. 

If, however, you would like to ensure your  talk or presentation has an impact, creates  change and enables people to work or think in  different ways… if you want to inspire others,  

enable them to understand ideas rather than  details… then PowerPoint is your enemy.  Having spent 20 years helping speakers with  their presentations at events my advice is that  you should use it as a last resort. Maybe as a way  of structuring some simple headlines or images  around which you will then talk with passion  and conviction.  

Sadly PowerPoint is the first port of call  for 90% of the world’s business leaders and  managers. How many times do you see the  familiar looking slide decks scroll up at a  conference or meeting or on someone’s laptop  in the train or in a coffee shop? Many business  managers use PowerPoint in the same way that  your dullest school teacher would have used an  overhead projector or your least favourite uncle  would present pictures of his canal holiday;  slide after slide, lock gate after lock gate\!  

By talking at an audience from the front there  is the deluded assumption that the information  has now been *communicated* or even ‘cascaded’.  ‘Surely now I have finished my 50+ slide  presentation everyone understands it, believes  it and is inspired as a result?’ We all know this  isn’t true because most of us have been in that  darkened conference room, maybe some of us  have even been that PowerPoint soldier at the  front too. 

The reality is that slick doesn’t make a  presentation good, sexy doesn’t make it credible  and full doesn’t make it complete. Our first  advice when coaching or helping presenters is to  step back from the slides and think about their  communication as a 20 minute conversation.  We advise them to try to have that conversation  with a colleague or coach and get them to write  the essence of it down on a bit of paper, on a    
tablecloth, on the wall … anywhere rather than  into some slides. 

The listener needs to probe into what exactly  are the key things that the presenter wants  their audience to remember, understand and  change? Keep distilling, keep asking until  you are sure that the essence is clear. Why not  embrace some old school technology to do this.  Write the presentation points down on a piece  of A4, one page per thought. Lay the A4s out  around the desk and then reshuffle them into a  running order with a clear beginning, middle  and end. 

![]()*Using the boardroom table to lay out a presentation.* 

Once you’ve done this you have your key  messages. There will be too many so reduce  them again. Ideally by the time you are finished  there will be no more than 12 pieces of paper  on the table. 

Now you can build and think about what ideas/ examples/illustrations would help bring these  to life. You may (only now) want to use some  

twenty four *www\.thevamagazine.com* 

PowerPoint slides to do this. Or alternatively  you may want to use some boards, pictures,  video clips or even an interview with (hold the  front page) a human being. A combination of  six to ten slides works quite well with other  illustrations as long as they contain no more  than headlines.  

Don’t be tempted to cram your slides with  lots of information in a reduced text size. Just  summarise in simple phrases the key message  that you wrote down originally. If you need  some prompts to present from then they are  better on A5 cards than on your slides. Don’t  make the cardinal sin of using PowerPoint  as your speaker notes as you will end up just  reading them and the audience will wish that  you’d stayed at home and just emailed them  the deck. 

Think of each of your 6-10 key points in terms  of mini stories with beginnings, middles and  ends. If possible, actually tell stories. Make those  stories *real,* amusing and even self deprecating  if it makes your point real. Certainly make  them relevant to your audience. Think about  your audience. Three times. The message is for  them. How do they best receive information?  Don’t worry about the fact that there will be  a bit of a rough edge in there. A PowerPoint  saturated audience will lap up your reality. 

The best presentations are not actually the slick  ones. Unless you are a naturally gifted orator  or stand up comedian they are also not side  splittingly funny. It’s hard to communicate  business messages at the best of times, but  making them into a stand up routine can  backfire badly. However a dose of humour and  the odd mistake enables the audience to relate  to the natural delivery of your messages and for  them to have meaning.  

Unless you have a load of charts and figures the  6-10 slides you will use should just be a few  images and some very simple statements. If  your messages rely on charts then think again  about presenting them in PowerPoint. Instead  hand them out, use templates, break the  audience into small groups at tables, get them  to fill in the blanks and to interact with your  

*about the author*   
*The Events Masterclass* 

data and content, to have discussions. Why not  make it a bit of a competitive task or test? Stay  away from the complex charts on screen which  your audience may be able to see (if you’re  lucky) but certainly can’t get involved with. 

Think of turning your content into a series  of experiences or interactions which could be  filmed (talking heads style) or even delivered  live by colleagues or possibly actors. We all have  the technology in our pockets through mobile  devices to create video or audio content, which  is certainly more engaging than PowerPoint  slides. Use tools such as iMovie to build these  clips into a meaningful story or get one of the  more tech savvy types in the office to help you.  Everyone loves to help make a movie, even if it’s  only about your new strategic direction. One of  the benefits of ‘YouTube’ is that it has lowered  audience expectations and helpfully reduced  the need for high production values for film. 

Use the physical space within which you are  presenting to tell your stories. We’ve graffitied  walls in warehouses and workshops, lined  offices with paper and drawn the stories and  ideas, walked delegates around A1 posters in a  conference space, even filmed a set of post cards  that were under the audiences seats and that,  when put together, completed the narrative  of the presentation. The trick is to get the  audience involved, as all the best shows do.  

The opening and closing ceremonies of the  Olympics were great examples where the  audience was actually involved in creating the  experience and therefore felt very much part of  it. This is memorable, this is lasting. I was lucky  enough to be at the opening ceremony and I  can remember how I *felt* and what I *thought* at  every stage. What I can’t remember, however,  are the key points from the PowerPoint heavy,  headline speech at an industry conference I  went to last week. 

But if you must use some form of slide  technology some of the more formal alternatives  to PowerPoint are as follows but remember  they too come with the same health warning. A  deck of slides, no matter how slick the format  can never replace the simplicity of a few clear  

messages and some well told/simply illustrated  stories. 

One of the best known PowerPoint alternatives  is Keynote which can be bought as a stand alone product, or as part of Apple’s iWork  productivity suite. Like PowerPoint it is  intuitive and easy to use and creates nice  looking presentations.  

Another is the Google Docs presentations  model recently updated to make it more  compatible with Microsoft Office. Google Docs  offers a wealth of templates and presentation  creation tools that are easy to jump in and get  started with; animations and slide transitions  to make your presentation interesting to watch;  and even collaborative presentation editing  with other people on your team 

And finally there is Prezi which is free and takes  presentations in a different direction away from  static slides on a screen or projector with text  on them. Even the animations and transitions  available in other slideshow applications pale in  comparison to Prezi’s “zooming user interface,”  which puts entire slides in motion and focuses  heavily on images, graphics, and motion to  draw attention to text.  

But at the end of the day these options are still  just flashy presentation formats that are no  substitute for the rich and relevant reality of  telling good stories, clearly with the minimum  of aid and the maximum of power. None of  the great speakers in history (or the present)  from Mandela to Churchill, from Clinton to  TED from JC to Dr ML King use(d) anything  other than stories in our minds to make their  messages understood. They didn’t need to add  the ‘Power’ to their Point and nor should you.  Think again. Take out some paper. Map it out.  Distill it. Rehearse and fly *PowerPoint free.* Be  different. Be real.  

Contact us if you need help, email me on lorne@ashdownacademy.com  

Lorne Armstrong 

Director, Ashdown Academy

*Communication* 

**Three Reasons Why  Business Owners  Are Not Hiring YOU  as Their Virtual**  

**Assistant** 

**Ever wondered why you’re not getting hired?**  

WRITER 

Cindy 

Greenway 

**Have you had fabulous conversations with a potential client, who you  knew desperately needed your help but they never called you and they  never hired you? Or perhaps you’ve built a great relationship with  a business owner but they hired someone else to help them in their  business?**

Over the years I’ve had the privilege of building  strong relationships with all kinds of business  owners. Through conversations with these  entrepreneurs, I’ve been fortunate to speak  candidly and listen to their opinions, wants,  expectations and obstacles when it comes to  working with a Virtual Assistant.  

So this article is for you, the VA, to help you  realise that the onus is on you to ask specific  questions and share specific details with  potential clients, so that then they can see  clearly that you are the best VA for them.  

The responsibility here lies with the VA  because, quite honestly, business owners don’t  know what they don’t know. This is not to  discredit or disrespect business owners but they    
have candidly shared with me that  they don’t know: 

•  what a VA can TRULY do  for them  

•  how a VA can impact the  growth and success of their  business 

•  where to find the right VA •  why some of their  expectations of a VA are  unrealistic 

•  when is the right time to  hire a VA 

•  what are the qualities of  the right VA for them 

twenty six *www\.thevamagazine.com* 

These are the questions that you, the Virtual  Assistant, must provide to a potential client. 

Here are three reasons why business owners  may not be hiring you, even after you have  connected and built a relationship with them.  You’ll see that in each example, a very simple  solution is available but it relies on your ability  to provide the information, on your own.  

(1) You haven’t proven that you understand  their business and their goals. 

I tell VAs all the time about a ‘secret  ingredient’ – it’s called ‘Business Mindset’.  Business Mindset is the ability gather enough  information so you can tell potential clients  how you can help them with their specific  business goals.  

When you apply this secret ingredient to your  day-to-day conversations with potential clients,  you gain an incredible opportunity to share  exactly how working with you will get them  closer to achieving the goals they have for their  business.  

When a VA says things like “I provide  administrative support to business owners so  they save time and stress.” or “I can help take  care of all the little details in your business so  you can do what you love most.” it sounds great  … but they still really have no idea what you  do or how you can help them.  

When you can relate specifically to their  business, their needs and their goals, the  light bulb will turn on and they’ll feel that  you understand them. If you connect strongly  specifically to the areas that will help them  build and make more money, they will feel  even more excited about working with you  right away.  

Think about your own work and business.  When you feel someone understands you and  your unique needs, don’t you feel more strongly  connected to them? You start to trust them  more, you think “yes, you’re the one I want.”  

*about the author*   
*Communication* 

That’s the same feeling you want a potential  client to feel about you.  

(2) You haven’t proven your value \--- Are you  an expense or an investment? 

Being a business owner can be tough … one of  the first things we do when money gets tight,  is look at our expenses and see where we can  cut costs.  

Some business owners will see their VA as an  expense that can be cut. They rationalise: “I  don’t really have the extra time to take care of  the admin pieces but I can save money here and  do it myself.” 

Other business owners will cut costs in all areas  before they even think about cutting back on  their VA. Why? Because they know that they  can’t truly grow (or even function\!) without the  support of their VA. 

What’s the difference between these two  situations? 

When I started in 2003, business was somewhat  uncomplicated compared to what it is today.  A VA with solid administrative skills who  managed the ‘backend’ of the business was of  incredible value. 

However, with all the new technology and  advancements in business, the ‘basics’ are  just not enough anymore. A business owner  does not always recognise the value in basic  administrative tasks anymore and therefore,  because it’s not supporting the growth of the  business, they see it as an expense. 

A VA who supports a business owner with  strategies and activities directly connected to  the growth of a business, will be viewed as a  investment and not an expense.  

(3) The business owner is simply nit ready to  hire a VA 

You will speak to many business owners who  think they are ready to hire a Virtual Assistant  

but many of them, regardless of what they  think, are simply NOT ready.  

The danger in this is that even though the pit of-your-stomach is telling you that this client  may not be the right one for you, you will still  agree to work with them, if they ask you to. 

One of the questions I encourage VAs to ask  all potential clients is “Tell me about your  business goals.” 

If a business owner answers this question with  anything like, “I want to find more clients,  make more money and be the go to person in  my area of expertise.” – they are probably not  ready for a VA. 

A business owner must have a clear vision of  their business goals and how they’re going to  reach those goals. It’s in the HOW that you  provide value. Through discussion with them,  you can brainstorm the strategies that will be  implemented to move towards this goal and  identify which tasks you will take on and which  your client needs to take on.  

If your potential client does not have a clear  vision of their business goals, other than to  simply find more clients and make more money,  then they are not ready to have you join them. 

Don’t take on ownership in feeling it’s your  responsibility to determine HOW you will  find more clients for them or HOW to help  them make more money – as the VA this is not  your role, this is a role of a Coach.  

When I’ve run into this situation with a  potential client, I’ve told them that in order  for me to best support them and contribute to  their growing business, I need more of HOW  they want to accomplish finding more clients  and making more money. I encourage them to  speak with a Coach and, once they have clearly  identified their goals, to reconnect with me so  we can take next steps.

Cindy Greenway, author of *Virtually Successful: 8 Simple Ways for Virtual Assistants to Find (and Keep) Clients,* provides  training and coaching to Virtual Assistants in-the-making as well as VAs who want to expand their current business.  She is inspired to help others create a rewarding business with great clients\! Since starting her own business in 2003,  Cindy has worked with well known online based business owners in the area of online marketing management. She  has also personally helped business owners identify their own needs and assisted them in finding virtual support team  members to support them. *Finance* 

**Planning for Profit Why all businesses need financial management**

WRITER   
John   
Palmer 

Now is a great time to start and grow a new  Virtual Assistant’s business. Cloud computing  and virtual working are extinguishing  major physical overheads like office space;  entrepreneurs are looking to cut employment  costs and so are more inclined to give work to  contractors and freelancers; and last, but not  least, businesses worldwide have realised the  power of social media. This latter development  has opened up a new revenue stream for  VAs who are now regularly hired to write or  proofread blog posts, update Twitter, Facebook  and LinkedIn pages and to comment on forums. 

However, while it may seem like a good  idea, starting a business is much easier than  keeping it going and keeping it in profit.  According to research from Harvard Business  School, between 30% and 40% of all new  US businesses fail before they reach their  

fifth anniversary. The UK’s failure figures are  similarly pessimistic according to a new report  from the Department for Business Innovation  & Skills. This states that of the 400,000 new  businesses that start in the UK every year,  one-third fail before year three. The Office for  National Statistics attributes start-up failure to  widespread poor financial management: 44%  of new businesses either run out of cash or come  very close to doing so and more than 40% of  new business owners say that knowing what  records to keep and “staying on top of it all” is  the biggest financial challenge they face. 

Like many other business owners, VAs are  strong on service delivery but often struggle  when it comes to planning and managing  financial matters and this can be the difference  between your own success and failure. But what  is financial management, exactly?    
Financial management is a framework of  set processes that a business uses to monitor,  organise and direct its income (the fees earned  from the services you provide), expenses  (electricity, telephones, cost of training etc)  and assets (the cash in your business bank  account and the money that you are owed with  the objectives of creating profit and ensuring  long-term sustainability. It is accomplished  through financial planning (especially before  the business is launched), through setting  up financial controls to monitor business  performance and through making informed  and effective financial decisions. In short, it is  about managing your business finances in such  a way that your business works towards making  a profit. 

If you think that financial management is for  big companies only and has nothing to do  

twenty eight *www\.thevamagazine.com* 

with starting and running a VA business then  consider the results of recent VA industry  surveys. These show that only a third of VAs  have relevant business training or experience  of running a business. Therefore, perhaps it is  unsurprising that, as we have mentioned in a  previous article, many who enter the profession  each year leave it after only 12 to 24 months.  In fact, our research at Be My VA shows that  the population of professional VAs has fallen  by almost 50% in the last two to three years,  particularly in the USA and the UK. Many  VAs appear to struggle to make it a long-term,  viable business and weak financial planning and  management skills are likely to be a significant  part of that problem. 

**Financial planning** 

Planning is crucial before you launch your  VA business – we all know the meaning of ‘If  you fail to plan, you plan to fail.’ Start with  your goals – what are they? For example, do  you want to be a fee income of say £60,000 or  £100,000 per year? If this is your goal, how do  you get there?  

Just like all new start-ups a VA will need to  research the market and assess potential demand  for their services. How many clients will you  need and how will you price your services to  reach the above figure and timescale? How  many hours a week will you need to work?  What is your financial outlay during this time?  For example, will you need to buy software or  enrol on a specific training course to develop  one or more of your skills? 

Consolidate all this research into a written  business plan incorporating financial forecasts  for say, two years ahead, if this is the timescale  you choose to work to. It will help you see any  gaps in your research and planning, and will  act as a benchmark against which to measure  the performance of your business after launch.  It will also show you if or when you can afford  to leave your day job and operate as a self employed VA full-time, or if you need to be  looking at finding additional finance to fund  your start-up.  

*about the author*   
*Finance* 

Be realistic when estimating your projected  revenue income turnover over the first two  years, especially if you will need to build up  your client base from scratch. Also, before you  leave your day job, ensure you have set aside  an emergency fund that will allow you to  pay your bills for a few months (6 months is  recommended).  

Your business plan should be backed up by a  marketing plan \- how will you get the clients  that you need to achieve the projected turnover?  Only then are you ready to implement  both plans and monitor them making any  adjustments if and when necessary. 

**Financial control** 

Hopefully you can now see that monitoring  how your business is doing is only possible if  you can measure this against something and  this something is your business plan and your  financial forecasts. But how do you know if you  are on target to achieve your goals? And what  should you be doing differently if you have  steered off course? 

The most important thing you can do to  make sure you stay ‘on course’ is to manage  your cash-flow in such a way that will allow  you to stay in business \- businesses fail when  they run out of cash. So, you want to maximise  your income and keep your business costs to a  minimum. How do you do this, day-to-day? Do  you bill your clients and chase unpaid invoices  regularly? Have you established a procedure for  dealing with late payments from clients? Are  you claiming all the allowances you can against  taxation? Do you budget for and keep track of  all business related expenses? 

One of the difficulties of being a ‘solopreneur’  is knowing how to live on an income that  will fluctuate, at least until you are better  established in business. It is therefore critical  that you know exactly how much money you  have coming in and going out (this includes  personal expenses as well as business expenses)  so that you can cut out any non-essential  expenditure. Cutting out such extras does not  

mean that you will have to do so forever, but  you will probably have to make some sacrifices  in the early days. 

To know how much money you have coming  in and going out, you will need to do some  bookkeeping. This can be done in Excel or with  an accounting software program. From this,  financial statements can be produced, such as  a balance sheet (a statement of your assets such  as your cash in the bank and of your liabilities)  and a profit and loss account (a statement of  your fee income, expenses and profits/losses). It  is by comparing the numbers on your original  financial forecasts to the numbers in these  financial statements that you will be able to  determine whether your business is growing  at the rate you had planned. You can then use  this insight to make financial decisions such  as whether you need to change your pricing  structure to increase profits or if you should  chase your debtors more vigorously to increase  your cash levels. 

Another aspect of financial control and cash  management is keeping personal and business  money separate. The best way to ensure this  is to have a separate bank account for your  business income and expenses. 

Keeping track of time worked and of the  invoices raised also comes under ‘financial  control’ \- how much time you have spent on  each client, how much you are owed, which  invoices are overdue. If you have a good system  in place for this purpose (there are a number  of web apps and reasonably priced software  options), it will allow you to easily and quickly  see where you are on client billing at any  given point in time, which is not as easy if you  manage invoicing manually. 

In future articles I will write more about  financial planning(business plan and cashflow  forecasts; financing your VA business; pricing  your services) and financial control (book 

keeping and time-tracking; billing and getting  paid; keeping track of business expenses).

John Palmer MA C.Dir is the founder and CEO of BeMyVA.com, a new innovative, international directory of Virtual  Assistants John is a Chartered Director and holds a Masters in Company Direction and is also founder and CEO of exec  4 – consultants and mentors to business leaders, based in London, UK since 1999\. *BeMyVA.com* has been developed as  an “independent, international directory” for locating professional Virtual Assistants worldwide. Our primary business  mission is to actively promote the value, skills and resources of Virtual Assistants to the business community at large. 

*www\.thevamagazine.com* twenty nine   
*Networking* 

**The Essential Tools to**  

**Networking Success: The Big  Question: What do you do? So many times people have said to me “Oh yes I have met**    
**a VA in the networking group but when I asked them  what they do they said ‘I am a Virtual Assistant’. I didn’t  quite understand.” When people don’t understand the  concept then they don’t really listen to you or they make an  assumption about you.**   
WRITER 

Carmen 

MacDougall 

It is really important that you are making a  good first impression in what you say because  first impressions do count and you only get one  opportunity to make a good first impression.  So, create a clear introduction about who you  are, what you do. 

Now I am sure you have heard of the eight or  ten second elevator pitch? This is when you  imagine you are in an elevator or a lift with  

somebody and you have eight seconds to tell  them what you do. Eight seconds isn’t very  long is it? At a networking meeting you will  probably have forty seconds or a minute to tell  people. So think how much you can convey  about you and what you do. 

What I have heard quite a few times is: “I  am a Virtual Assistant and I do your diary  management for you. I can help you manage    
your work so you have got more time and more  money.” I have even heard: “I can help you  manage your family\!”  

The response is: “Gosh\! What is that?” or  “What do you charge for that?”  

If the VA does have the confidence to talk about  their fees and says “£25 per hour” then the  response will be: “To do my diary management  you will charge me £25 per hour? Wow that’s  expensive.”  

This comes across as if you are selling and  doesn’t give people the opportunity to  understand what you actually do. 

So, what do you do? You are not aiming  the answer to the person who has asked the  question. You are not saying “I can do your diary  management for you, I can do your document  management, I can manage your emails for  you” because that is making the assumption  that these are the services they want and need  and you must be very careful about making  assumptions. To the person that said they could  help to manage my family, I said: “Where on  my badge does it say I have got children?”  

When someone says “What do you do?” tell  them what you do. Focus on who you work  closely with, what solutions you provide and  what outcomes your clients receive. It’s simple  because it’s based on what you do: “I provide  business and social media support. Helping  my clients to raise their profile, so that they  can focus on generating better relationships  with their existing clients, which creates more  business, which leads to increased income.”

thirty *www\.thevamagazine.com* 

Think about what’s in it for them and talk  about the benefits. 

If you say that you can manage someone’s diary,  what does that actually mean? It’s amazing how  many people don’t understand the value and  say: “What’s the point of having a VA to do  that when I can do it myself?” 

You need to be clear about your introduction  and remember that people will ask questions to  help them understand more. When you answer  those questions you have the opportunity to  expand on your introduction. 

So if someone asks: “Well how can you help  me?” then ask them questions to determine  what support you can offer them and to  establish what their pain factors are. I would  be suggesting solutions and explaining the  positive impact on their business. Here are  some good examples to start you off: 

•  What is an hour of your time worth to  your business?  

•  In the last week, what took up your  valuable time and prevented you from  actually achieving part of your 90 Day  Plan?  

•  Is there anything in particular that you are  working on? 

•  Have you found that there have been  interruptions to working on that project?  •  What were those interruptions?  •  When you are in bed at night what makes  you feel anxious about the business for the  next day?  

•  What is keeping you awake at night?  

When you have asked these questions you can  be really clear about what you do in terms of  delivering benefits to their business. A lot of  VAs talk about the benefits being: “you don’t  have to employ somebody, you don’t have any  overheads or training or equipment, you only  pay for the time that I do your work.” These  are features or standard benefits but these are  not the benefits of working with you\! Explain  what it is about you that is individual and be  very clear about what’s in it for them by them  working with YOU\! 

*about the author*   
*Networking* 

Be clear about who you support. If you work  with a niche market \- tell people that. Don’t  suddenly say “I work with everybody” out of fear  that you are going to lose out on an opportunity.  You might be talking to an accountant and  have made a fantastic connection with them  but your niche market are trainers in the  pharmaceutical industry. If the accountant says:  “Well I know I am not in the pharmaceutical  industry but I would love to work with you  because I love your energy, you are very clear  about the benefits of working with you and  that’s what I want for my business.” Then you  can take that client on board because you have  demonstrated those qualities. 

When VAs have a clear niche market they  are remembered. When you talk about who  you support and you explain what sort of  support you are seeking for yourself people will  remember that. So by saying what you do, in  a clear way, you will be seen as different. It’s  OK to talk about yourself, it’s absolutely OK to  talk about yourself. You are not selling unless  you start saying “I can do this for you, I can do  that for you.” 

All that the person wants to know is: What  you do? Who do you do it for? How do you  do it? What are the client’s financial benefits?  How do you have that positive impact on their  income? How do you boost their income?  

It’s OK because you are there to tell people  about your business. You are also there to listen  and support others. So listen more than you  talk, let the other person feel valued. Create an  opportunity where you can offer support, show  that you are really listening and you are not  thinking “They are going to ask me what I do  next and I don’t know what to say\!” 

You are listening for an opportunity where  you can make a connection for them. A referral  that you can make for them or perhaps an  introduction to someone in the room. Listen  out for that opportunity. There might be  someone, just that right person you are looking  for, who could be that future strategic partner  or the connector to your niche market. 

Listen and ask good questions that will generate  good responses. You can prepare these questions  so think about some non-standard ideas, you  want something that is going to get people to  talk not just give “yes” or “no” responses. This  will start to build their trust in you because you  are taking an interest in them. 

Active listening skills are worth their weight  in gold. It is really important that you listen  because then you will hear that opportunity  and you are making that person feel they want  to get to know you as well. 

This is the tip of the iceberg and there is an  awful lot more that I would love to share with  you but these are the 3 key areas:  

•  You have worked on your strategy.  •  You have worked on your confidence and  you have worked on what you want to say.  •  You have built all of this into your 90 Day  Plan.  

You are now going to experience far more  impact and return on investment in your  networking. I have coached and trained so  many VAs who have followed these rules and  have got their return on investment and so have  been renewing their membership  

Invest in your networking and give it time.  If you are not seeing immediate results think  about what you need to do differently. It is an  ongoing process and you have to be consistent  with it. The more people that you know and  you build relationships with and make the  effort to meet for one-to-ones and show your  support for and get to know at a different level,  then you are going to start seeing results. 

I really do hope you are going to take action  and make a commitment because if you don’t  see a return on your investment, I will eat my  hat\! 

Carmen MacDougall came from a corporate background as a PA and Office Manager before establishing her VA business  over 13 years ago after redundancy. She has since established herself as one of the leading trainers in how to launch and  develop a successful Virtual Assistant business through The VA Coaching and Training Company *www\.vact.co.uk* using  a model which has helped many of her clients to become award winning VAs. She founded The national  Va Conference and awards in 2007 and has recently been appointed as Director of Training for The International  Association of Virtual Assistants. Carmen was very proud to be chosen for the Spirit of the VA Industry award in 2011  due to her passion and support in putting the VA industry on the map.

**From Assistant to CEO** 

**Have you been in a situation where everything  seems to be going well? Then BAM\! The balance  is upset and you have to face a change. How do  you deal with it?**

WRITER   
Rona Cant 

![]()  
Imagine a scenario where your job has  disappeared, although the process will be the  same in any situation, this is the moment when  you need to stop, take a step back and look  at the bigger picture. What is the worse that  could happen? Is it life threatening? It may feel  as though it is at that particular moment but it    
probably isn’t. It may well be challenging but  you have met challenge before and know you  can overcome it.  

If you have been prudent you will have been  putting some money into savings for just this  sort of situation or you may get a redundancy    
payment. You might have a month or so  before your job disappears completely so you  have extra time to work out a plan of action.  Do you want the security of being employed  by an organisation or is this the moment to  provide your own security by running your own  business? 

thirty two *www\.thevamagazine.com* 

It is an ideal time to take control and take on  this challenge. With the present economic  situation there has been a rise in the number of  small businesses that have been created. They  may start off as one person working from home  but as they build their business they will need  someone to help them and a Virtual Assistant  is exactly what they need. So you will become  a small business supporting maybe one or two  slightly bigger businesses but that is how all  businesses start. How far you want to take your  business is entirely up to you. You could grow  it as big as you want by organising other VAs to  work for other businesses.  

So how would you start? This is where you  plan what you are going to do and how you are  going to do it. Some of the questions you need  to address will relate to the particular industry  with whom you want to work, what you will  charge, what equipment you will need if you  are working from home, what effect will this  decision make on other parts of your life? But  the most important question is this one: what  is your vision for the future? Where do you  want your new business to take you? What will  your life look like once you have accomplished  that goal? 

This is not a process that can happen quickly.  You need to take time, put the idea into your  head and let it take you wherever it decides to  go, there are no boundaries. Once you have seen  the bigger picture you can work out how you  will achieve the goal. Do not allow your mind  to be bound by unfounded preconceptions. 

Let me explain exactly what I mean. I run my  own business helping organisations with their  leadership and teamwork or helping them  overcome various obstacles and challenges by  using the lessons I have learnt from various  expeditions and from my life. So I always speak  from those experiences.  

After we successfully completed The Nordkapp  Expedition I set up the Arctic Leadership  Challenges working with the leader of the  Expedition. We take groups or individuals into  the Arctic Circle dog-sledding in the winter  and sailing, kayaking, orienteering and rock 

*about the author*   
*Personal Development* 

climbing in the summer. The Arctic Leadership  Challenges are my legacy. I set it up so that it  can be run by my children in the future and it  will provide another revenue stream for them or  they can sell it to someone else. 

So find yourself a comfortable place, turn off  the telephone and with pad and pen in hand  write down what you would like your life to  look like in 5, 10, even 15 years. It may take  some time to draw out exactly what you will  be doing but stay with it. Once you have that  vision it is time to turn your thoughts to what  you will need to do to get you there. 

You will have to decide where your office will  be – it’s a good idea to start with a room at  home that you can close off at the end of the  day. What equipment will you need for your  office and to back up your business? What sort  of telephone system will you install? What  insurance will you need? The list goes on.  When I started my business a friend suggested  that I should read a very good book called *The  E-Myth Revisited* by Michael E. Gerber which  shows you how to set up a business that can be  replicated over and over again but still keep the  same model as the original. 

There’s lots of useful information on the  internet and organisations such as Business  Link, Chambers of Commerce and your bank  who can advise you. Start going to networking  events in your area, not only will this bring  you into contact with possible future clients  but also with people who can advise you on  marketing your business. You will meet other  business owners and one of them could become  your mentor. We all need help and not only  when we are starting up our business.  

As an assistant you have been used to working  for someone else and in the terms of the Belbin  Team Roles you would most probably be a  ‘Completer Finisher’. You probably have very  high standards that head towards perfection  with details checked time and time again. You  probably focus on the minor details and do not  delegate tasks to others as you are not really  sure that you trust them to work to your high  standards. 

That is admirable when you are working for  someone else but not when you are working for  yourself. Don’t get me wrong, you still need to  provide work of a high degree but there will  only be you doing it at the beginning. However  the most important phrase you need to know  and understand is ‘Cash is King’. I know it  sounds wrong but, without it, you are not  going to put food on the table or pay any of  your bills. 

You will need to hone your ‘Plant’ skills  (another Belbin Team Role), the creative,  unorthodox side of you. You may have to ignore  incidentals and you cannot afford to concentrate  on the detail because as the business owner you  have now become, you have to be innovative to  be able to solve the challenges that come your  way. And there will be challenges but you have  overcome them before and you will do again. 

You will also have to look to your ‘Shaper’ skills  where you pursue your goals with tremendous  energy. So you need to focus on the money and  as your business grows then you must provide  the drive that will keep other VAs (if that is  what you decide to do) focussed. As a Shaper it’s  up to you to challenge your team to improve,  to stimulate them and find the best way to  overcome those challenges. You will have to  look at each problem from every angle to find  the right solution and you will need to keep  going when others want to quit. 

It’s really helpful to have a mastermind group to  keep you focussed, to challenge you to achieve  your goals and to provide advice if necessary. 

The most important thing is to chase the money  by doing a really good job and delivering what  you say you will deliver on time, within budget  and to a good standard.  

Is it easy? No. Is it challenging? Yes. Is it  rewarding? It certainly is. People are generous  with their help and knowledge so don’t be  afraid to ask for help. Where will your business  take you? That’s for you to decide\!

Rona Cant is an explorer, international speaker and author. Once an unexceptional, middle-aged single parent she  became a round-the-world yachts woman by competing in the BT Global Challenge Round the World Yacht Race (the  toughest yacht race in the world). For the last 10 years she has been putting together programmes on leadership and  teamwork, and motivating businesses and individuals to get out of their comfort zone and reach their potential based on  the lessons learnt on her expeditions. Her second book, Snow, Sleds & Silence – the Story of the Nordkapp Expedition  describes the ups and downs of her epic adventure on this trail, which lead her to establish Arctic Leadership Challenges  Ltd, which offers experiential training inside the Arctic Circle for individuals and teams.

*Personal Development* 

**Errors are not the Enemy\!** 

WRITER   
Marsha Egan 

If there’s one thing most of us hate to admit to,  that we try to hide and put a good deal effort  into trying to minimise, it’s our errors. I’ve  never really understood why. After all, we’re all  human. So, by that very fact, we’re all imperfect  and we all make mistakes. It’s one of the things  that binds us all together as the human race  – the fact that we all make mistakes. When  we make a mistake, the first thing most of us  does is either pretend it didn’t happen, blame  someone else or seek to justify it. Some people  go in the opposite direction and beat themselves  up over it unmercifully, but the majority seeks  to minimise the perception of the fault.  

The thing about mistakes is that they don’t  exist until they have been made, which means  that we’re only aware of them by definition  once they are already in the past. For this reason  you can’t change mistakes, which means that  self-flagellation over an error is pointless – it  achieves nothing except to make us feel worse  by hitting us where it hurts – in pride and  self-worth. However, while giving ourselves  a hard time over mistakes made is pointless,  pretending the mistake never happened or  seeking to gloss over it is a missed opportunity. 

You may think that sounds crazy but,  our mistakes are one of the best personal  development resources we have available\!  Being actively part of something which didn’t  work is a major learning experience if you allow  it to be.  

Firstly, these aren’t just anyone’s mistakes, they  are ours. This means they are not just specific  to your career or lifestyle, but also specific to  our personal way of doing things and thought  

*about the author* 

processes. No-one else will make the same  mistake in exactly the same way. I’ll bet you  won’t do the same thing again, particularly if  you pay attention to how the mistake came  about in the first place – using it as a learning  curve. We’re all creatures of habit, and the same  situation will almost certainly happen again,  only with a wiser you at the helm. 

Secondly, congratulations, you’ve just received  a first-hand practical lesson in ‘how not to do  it’. There’s something especially beneficial to  learning by personal experience. It’s like the  difference between reading a book about how  to change a car’s oil and getting a one-on-one  master class from a great mechanic. No book  can better experience as an educator. And  learning how to do something right by doing it  wrong is a hard educational experience to top. 

Thirdly and finally, you’ll have had to fix your  error. This cements the lesson. By participating  in the recovery, you’ll have analysed the error  in detail, even if it was subconscious. In fact,  you’ve done far more analysis than you would  have done if you got it right the first time.  

So, when we have such a strong case for the  concept that our mistakes may be our best  teachers, why do so many people ignore these  outstanding learning opportunities? 

1\. Unwillingness to accept fault: We’ve all  seen it – there are people who will simply  refuse to admit that they were wrong, even  in the face of overwhelming evidence.  Whatever their reasoning, their refusal to  admit that they were wrong is a barrier to  learning from the situation. 

2\. Reluctance to admit their failings: A lot of  people think that holding your hands up  to making a mistake is a sign of weakness,  when in reality it’s quite the opposite.  The fact is that people admire those who  are secure and confident enough to admit  their mistakes.  

3\. Poor self image: Some people are sadly  so low in the self-image department that    
another ‘nail into the coffin’ is too much  to cope with. 

4\. Bad attitude: It’s hard for those of us who  take pride in our work to comprehend but  some people just couldn’t care. They aren’t  interested in learning from their mistakes  – they move but they are unfortunately at  risk of simply making the same mistake  again and again as they haven’t had the  benefit of that personal master class. 

5\. Lack of awareness: They may be aware of  their mistake but not aware of the benefits  of experiential learning, which means they  may not take advantage of that ‘master  class waiting to happen’. 

Each person’s reasons will be slightly different  but they are still potentially very expensive  excuses.  

Mistakes are a master class with the finest  teacher if we allow them to be and, far from  their purely negative connotation, they can be  a career and life-enhancing in the long-term.  

In order to benefit from this opportunity,  once you are aware of your error and after  you’ve corrected it, ask yourself the following  question: 

•  What have I learned from what I did  wrong? 

•  What could I have done differently, and  what would I do differently in future? •  What could I have done that would have  maximised the result? 

None of us are perfect and we all have and will  continue to make mistakes. Nothing you can  do will change this fact. The one thing you  can change is how you respond to it and how it  informs your future actions.  

Just remember, those who maximise the value  of their mistakes through learning from them  are far less likely to repeat them, leading to less  mistakes, and more success, in future\!  

Marsha Egan, CPCU, PCC, is CEO of the Egan Group, Inc., Reading PA. An ICF Certified Professional Coach, she is  a leading authority on email productivity. She works with forward thinking organizations who want a profit-rich email  culture.  

Her recently released book, *Inbox Detox and the Habit of Email Excellence* can be found at *http\://InboxDetox.com*

*Technology* 

**Technology Makes Success  Possible for Today’s Virtual  Assistant** 

WRITER 

Alicia 

Rittenhouse 

The virtual assistance industry is contributing to changing the way business  is conducted and the use of technology is one of the biggest contributions  but many Virtual Assistants are struggling to keep up because there is no  resource to teach them. VAs have strong research skills but without knowing  exactly what to search for it is challenging to find the right resources. 

Virtual Assistants are more than secretaries.  Your clients expect you to know how to support  every area of their business but you may feel  that you are limited in the help you can offer  as you do not have a degree in marketing or  web design or communications. However by  using the right technology you can actually  do all three roles. Think of the advantages of  being able to update a website without having  to learn how to be a coder or developing your  clients’ profile through social media or helping  them to gain press coverage on local, national  and international levels.  

All this is possible with technology. VAs are  great at getting tasks done and enjoy handling  a variety of tasks and responsibilities. However,  to be a success you need to be proactive and  ahead of the game in everything you do and  knowledge of technology will give you the  necessary edge in today’s market.  

The need for tech-savvy VAs is growing \- in  2010 aproximately half a million businesses  were started every month. Not every one of  those businesses could use a VA but there is an  abundance of potential clients out there who do  need our help. As well as great administrative  skills It is essential to be able to set up a client’s  newsletter and web forms, maintain their  website and market them through social media. 

Successful business owners focus on what they  are good at and what they sell and produce but  

*about the author* 

*![]()*there is the other side to business that involves  the ongoing marketing and buzz that is needed  to attract new customers. To be successful as a  VA you need to support the marketing of your  clients’ businesses.  

Here are a few ways you can fuse technology  with your role to make a marketing impact: 

1\. Online Payments – To be profitable, there  must be a way to be paid and this could  include setting up products, connecting  payment buttons and customising a  shopping cart. If your clients get paid then  you get paid. 

2\. Email Marketing – This is the most  effective way to market because it works\!  There are many email marketing services  out there and they allow your clients to  build a relationship with their prospects  and support sales. You need to know how  to integrate and segment using various  services. 

3\. Product Launches – It is not enough to  put up an offering on a website and expect  it to sell. Successful launches bring in the  money as they create sales, visibility and  credibility. A VA can support much of the  launch process. 

4\. Productivity – The more you learn the  better you manage your time and the more  work you can handle. Project management  tools are very useful as businesses grow  and the need for clear communication  increases. 

5\. WordPress – Be your clients’ webmaster.  Teach them how to turn their website into  a powerhouse using WordPress. Google  loves WordPress and a growing number of  websites use it as their platform. Here are  some famous WordPress sites: 

Mashable, CNN, The New York Times,  UPS, eBay, Sony and even Katy Perry. 

6\. Social Media – Everyone is doing it and it  is not going away. 

When you become the VA who can do all of  this, your clients will be more likely to commit  to a monthly retainer and you will quickly fill  your business. Embrace technology by learning  the skills prospective clients are looking for  and then you can increase your hourly rate and  the amount of time you work with each client  which will increase your income. 

Technology makes everything possible. 

Alicia Rittenhouse, Digital Diva and Tech Cheerleader. Her Geek Labs is a high-inspiration playground for Virtual  Assistants (brand new and experienced) to sharpen their skills \-- and double their rates \-- in just six weeks. Alicia has a  talent for breaking down technical mysteries into simple steps that even non-geeks can master.  

Geek out with Ali at *vatrainingprogram.com* and *alirittenhouse.com*.

*Marketing* 

**What I Learned  About Marketing  From My Cats**

|  |
| :---- |
|  |
|  |
|  |
|  |
|  |
|  |

thirty six *www\.thevamagazine.com* 

WRITER   
Jeannine Clontz 

I share my work environment with my two cats,  Zach and Zoe. They both have me very well  trained. First thing in the morning, I turn on  the computer and follow my talkative Siamese,  Zoe, into the kitchen to give her a special treat.  If it’s a weekend and I’m thinking that I might  like to sleep past 6:00 a.m., then Zach, the gray  tabby, is sure to be rubbing against me in bed,  licking my face and making sure that I know  it’s time for them to eat. 

During the day, before the extended catnaps  begin, they both take turns pacing back and  forth in front of my computer screen or plopping  down in front of my monitor making me all  too aware of their need of a good scratching  by using their irresistible purring. They really  know how to make me aware of their presence  and needs. 

It dawned on me this morning that they’re  really presenting me with their marketing  strategy. Just a few nudges, some cute kitty  kisses, a calming purr or two and I’m ready to  do their bidding. Their presentation is simply  flawless. They’ve learned what it takes to get  their desired results. 

In the last few months I have been stepping up  my social networking. I’ve included it in my  marketing plan so that I not only have specific  time designated for this type of marketing  strategy but I also manage the time I spend in  these networks.  

I’ve connected with people who seem to have  the same desires and passions as my own and  have been able to begin to build relationships.  It’s been much harder to do this ‘virtually’ than  it was to make face-to-face connections but I  am seeing the light at the end of the tunnel. 

So how do I get to the desired results? How  do I convert these relationships into money?  As with traditional marketing practices, it’s all  about getting people to know, like and trust  you. When I did monthly marketing postcards  I certainly didn’t get an immediate stream of  new business, so why would I think that with  

*about the author*   
*Marketing* 

just a few Tweets the business would start  pouring in? 

Since I started this marketing aspect several  positive things have happened: I had a nice  online radio interview to discuss my book; I  have had several requests for interviews; I’ve  been asked to contribute blog posts to groups  that include my target market; I have found  two service providers that I needed; I have been  approached by two people to meet outside the  social network to find out more about each  other; I’ve signed two clients and I have found  great resources and insights that have helped  me to develop my new coaching practice and  teleseminar series. 

I’ve enjoyed the ride especially as it was FREE\!  Apart from the time I used by participating  in these social networks, there has been no  actual out-of-pocket expense. Why have I been  waiting? If you’ve been participating in these  social networks for several months and just  don’t get it or if you’ve considered it but don’t  know how – here are some of the things I’ve  used successfully to develop and grow my social  presence and build some great relationships and  business resources that I know will transition  into new business. 

•  Be a problem solver – if someone posts  a question, needs a resource or just some  positive feedback then be that problem  solver. 

•  Make sure your profile is up-to-date and  that you constantly change and develop it  so people see the value in connecting with  you. 

•  If you don’t have a free-standing blog then  use some of the online social networks that  provide you with one and post articles and  resources that will give people an idea of  who you are, your level of expertise and  how you work. 

•  Be selective about the groups you join  and the friends you connect with – don’t  just ‘connect’ with everyone. Connect to  

people who seem to have the same values  and passions as you. 

•  Join groups within the network that will  have people within your niche or target  market. 

•  Be sure you designate a specific amount  of time, daily or weekly, when you check  in and do something – even if it’s just  adding a photo, blog or article, starting a  discussion, asking for help. You have to be  present in order to connect. 

•  Be yourself. Let your hair down a little –  don’t make it all about business, make it  a little personal, too. Talk about family,  kids, travel, hobbies, whatever makes you,  you\! 

•  Don’t make it all about your business. The  people I don’t accept connections to are  usually those whose sites say, ‘buy me’ and  nothing more. Most people don’t want to  be sold to, they want to make the buying  decision in their own way. Give them the  information they need to make the right  decision to fulfill that need. 

•  For sites like LinkedIn that offer you the  option of adding recommendations – use  them\! This is a great way for people to  get an idea of how valuable you and your  products/services are to others. Prospects  are much more likely to give you a try if  they see someone else has fulfilled a need  they have. 

•  Don’t go to other’s pages, blogs, or  listings and just post your offerings, this is  considered spam on just about every level. 

Social networking really is the wave of the  future and one that’s not going to go away any  time soon. So dip your toes in the water and  give it a try. And please be sure to connect with  me on: LinkedIn, Facebook, Twitter, Plaxo,  Google+ and growing.

Jeannine Clontz, provides professional business coaching to established and start-up Virtual Assistants (VA’s). *www\.thevamagazine.com* thirty seven   
*communication* 

**Are You On Your  Lonesome?**

WRITER   
Helen Jacobi 

![]()thirty eight *www\.thevamagazine.com* 

One of the main attractions when considering  a home-based business is the freedom and  independence it will bring you. Once the  decision is made and you’re on your way the  novelty of no longer having to commute is  refreshing and you generally have more time in  your day.  

However, in my experience, the novelty lasts  around 3 months. The initial driving factors  remain but finding yourself on your own most  days can begin to have a negative impact on  your business. Feelings of loneliness and  isolation start to kick in and we miss the  camaraderie we had in a conventional office  environment. The good news is it doesn’t have  to be this way – virtual businesses do not need  to operate in isolation. 

Whilst home working has plenty of upsides,  if you don’t create firm parameters then the  blessing can turn into a curse. Being out and  about networking is great but it doesn’t fully  compensate coming back home and being on  your own again. Here are some thoughts and  ideas on how to retain your independence  whilst creating a working environment that  keeps you motivated and sane.  

A neighbour of mine recently left the city  life and struck up in business as a brand  communications consultant. Whilst the appeal  of home working and having more flexibility  were amongst her motivations to go it alone,  she found it frustrating to work from home  without a proper office.  

Kitchen table working is great but it can lead  to distraction as well as not being able to easily  separate work life from home life. Likewise,  having a desk in the corner of the bedroom isn’t  conducive on a long term basis. My neighbour  didn’t want the overhead of hiring office space  at this stage, so I offered her the spare desk in  my office. No extra cost to me and it helps her  

*about the author*   
*communication* 

have a work space to call her own. We agreed a  trial period and haven’t looked back. 

Much to our surprise we didn’t talk much at all.  In fact, quite the opposite. We kept our heads  down and got on with our own work. Both of  us found it incredibly motivating to be in the  company of another business owner, purely and  simply by having someone else there. 

Moving on from sharing a home office with  someone, it can work out very competitively to  hire desk-space off-site and share the cost, even  for a day or two a week. Many serviced offices  offer this arrangement because they realise  businesses need a base, but not necessarily five  days a week.  

Equally, local companies often have spare  desks either due to down sizing or extra space  to expand into. Again, with a little savvy  negotiation, bargains are to be had and both  parties benefit. Plus you are likely to have  access to their in-house facilities such as wifi,  reception or meeting rooms. Be sure you have  an agreement in place setting out the terms and  the cost involved.  

When I need to be creative I like to change  my environment and get away from my home  office. When we’re running events in London,  I utilise a spare desk in a friend’s office to catch  up with client work.  

Another option to consider is joining a  members club that offers not only hot-desk  space and office facilities but also networking  and learning opportunities. Quite a few of the  more entrepreneurial clubs offer various levels  of membership, many with flexible terms and  no long term commitment. If you have a niche  client market, finding a club in that particular  industry is a fantastic place to meet potential  clients and keep up with industry changes. It’s  

good value too – networking and office space,  all under one roof.  

And finally, if a regular commitment isn’t your  thing perhaps consider hiring a meeting room  for a day every month or two and sharing the  cost amongst the sharers. This works really  well, particularly if you have a team that all  work remotely. It’s a great opportunity to get  together, work under one roof and thrash out  ideas.  

And finally, a few guidelines on making an  office buddy or shared work space work for you: 

•  Your businesses don’t need to be the same. •  Commit to being there on a regular basis,  even if it’s easy to stay at home that day.  It will help give you a focus and shape to  your diary. 

•  Agree at the outset to be completely  honest with each other if you find it isn’t  working. 

•  Establish the hours that you plan to share  the office – and stick to them. 

•  If there’s a cost involved, be up front and  agree what’s included and who pays for  what.  

•  Resist the temptation to talk\!  

Of course all of this is enabled by the amazing  amount of technology available that helps us  work remotely from wherever we choose.  

If you’re feeling the pang of being too alone  or isolated, consider who you could buddy up  with and what work space solutions might  work for you. 

Helen Jacobi has been an Executive Assistant for over 20 years working alongside boards in large corporations and with  successful entrepreneurs. In 2009 she took the plunge and started The Inspired PA, offering Virtual PA and association  management solutions to clients globally. Working on the basis that flexibility and adaptability are critical in  challenging economic times, the virtual business model struck a chord across various industries and types of businesses.  Earlier this year Helen won Outstanding VA of the Year for over 2½ years in business. 

*www\.thevamagazine.com* thirty nine   
*Personal Development* 

**What Morgan**  

**Freeman Taught Me**  

**About Goals** 

WRITER   
Rosie Gray 

Last weekend I watched the film *The Bucket List* and it reminded me of the whole goal setting  process and why it matters to us all. If you  haven’t seen the film \- it stars Jack Nicholson  & Morgan Freeman. Without spoiling the plot  \- two terminally ill cancer patients decide to  leave hospital, go on a round the world trip and  live their last days to the full. They’re intent  on experiencing as many things on their ‘wish  list’ as possible before they ‘kick the bucket’.  They called this their ‘bucket list’. (It’s not as  depressing as it sounds – I recommend it.) 

Now at the moment, I’m fit, healthy and  planning to stay that way and hope you are  too. But none of us knows what life has in store  and there are experiences I don’t want to regret  missing because I left it too late. 

So I’ve been updating my own list of dreams  this week. I first did this about 15 years ago  and been adding to it ever since. As I experience  the dream, I cross it off so there’s always room  for more.  

If you haven’t ever made such a list, I  recommend that you do – it’s very powerful.  What you call your list is up to you – maybe it’s  a ‘bucket list’ or ‘fifty things I want to do before  I’m fifty’ – call it whatever is meaningful for  you. I’ve always simply called mine my ‘dream  list’. 

But make it – it doesn’t need to take long.  Maybe you’ll make your list on the train going  home this evening or take 30 minutes on a  quiet Sunday morning while enjoying a cup of  good coffee.    
What Might Be On Your List? 

Your list will be unique to you. It reflects who  you are, your interests, your values and what  you’ve experienced to date. There is no ‘perfect’  or ‘right’ list. It will not be the same as mine  or your best friend’s, although there may be  similarities.  

Maybe you want to watch the sun rise while  sitting at the top of a mountain, learn to play  the saxophone or improve your golf handicap.  Just capture those things you would absolutely  love to do and would regret if you didn’t. 

Ask yourself: 

•  What have you always wanted to do and  haven’t yet?

forty *www\.thevamagazine.com* 

•  Are there any special events you want to  attend? 

•  If time, money or resources were unlimited  – what would you do? 

•  Who would you like to meet? 

•  Who would you like to have a better  relationship with? Family? Friends? 

•  Where in the world might you want to  visit? 

•  What hobbies, skills, sports would you  like to learn or try out? 

•  What would mean a great deal to you? •  If you knew you would die tomorrow, what  would you regret not having experienced? 

Write down as many ideas as you can. Keep  adding to it. 

There’s no pressure to do everything this year.  Your list will always be a ‘work in progress’ as  you cross through items achieved and think of  more. 

Most of the things on my own list wouldn’t  necessarily take much money or even time. But  all would give me tremendous pleasure. Here’s  a small selection to give you some ideas… 

•  Ride a horse through the waves along a  sandy beach   
*Personal Development* 

•  See the northern lights 

•  Visit the pyramids 

•  Learn pilates 

•  Listen to the absolute natural silence  found in a really wild place – perhaps the  Sahara or Antarctica?  

•  Pass on a skill I enjoy to a child •  Plant an orchard and one day enjoy the  fruits  

•  Revisit some of the European cities I  visited on a tight budget years ago 

•  Learn more about astronomy 

What Difference Will Your List Make To You? 

By giving yourself time and space to think  about the experiences you want to enjoy, you’ve  already increased the chance of making those  good things happen. 

By capturing these thoughts you’ll be more  likely to remember, see opportunities and act on  them. I believe many people (including myself)  limit the scale of our achievements by aiming  low, playing it safe. We set goals according to a  ‘world view’ of what seems possible for us, what  we can afford or have time to do. 

Writing a ‘bucket list’ begins to turn the  process around and leads to living life at its  

*‘The greatest danger is not that*    
*we aim too high & miss. It’s that*    
*we aim too low & reach it’* 

**Michelangelo** 

fullest. We’re asking instead what highlights  we want to experience in our lives and then  work out ‘how’ to make that happen. 

I know from my experience that this results in  setting very different goals. Goals and actions  are aligned with what is needed to achieve these  important experiences. Typically I’m much  more motivated because completing the actions  leads to something I really care about. I’ve been  surprised and delighted by how many things  I’ve experienced and crossed off my list. 

And as you read this you might be thinking:  “And when and how am I going to do all this,  Rosie? I have no time.”  

Well, the first thing is to decide that you want  to and to start capturing those ideas. You’ll be  surprised by the momentum and motivation  it’ll create. When you know why you’re doing  it (for example saving the money for your  big trip) even the dullest tasks become more  tolerable. 

So what will be on your list? I would love you  to share your exciting dreams \- You just might  remind me of something else to add to my list\! 

*‘You are never too old to set a new*    
*goal or dream a new dream’* 

**C.S. Lewis** 

*‘The world is your playground.*    
*Why aren’t you playing?’* 

**Ellie Katz**

*about the author*   
*‘All our dreams can come  true, if we have the  courage to pursue them’*  

**Walt Disney**
