*Business Strategy* 

**Tapping Into Your  Customers’ Tastes:  How Chocolate Has**  

**the Answers…** 

WRITER   
Claire Lister 

![]()  
I’ve been a tad obsessed by Chocolate Week this  year. Any excuse to eat chocolate is welcome and  I enjoy exploring new tastes and flavours. What  surprised me is that my indulgent month has  led me to thinking about business in a different  way. My varied chocolate experiences set me  

thinking on broader topics and I thought I’d  share the outcome. 

**Personal Taste** 

As my colleagues and I have been working our  way through different boxes, there have been    
many heated discussions about our favourites.  This got me thinking about personal taste. 

It reminded me of conversations I had with VAs  at Office\* recently, several people discussed  concerns around how to know what clients  really wanted. There were lots of questions 

forty two *www\.thevamagazine.com* 

around how to cater for everyone’s personal  taste because if you are offering a broad range  of services how do you know which to push?  How do you make potential clients choose you?  

The answer comes in understanding that you  can’t expect everyone to like the same things.  People want different things for different  reasons and in business we have to work with  this. How you deal with this comes back to  how you package yourself and where you price  yourself in the market. 

Consider your services as chocolates: a mixture  of many shapes and sizes. 

**We All Have Our Favourites** 

Imagine your family sitting on the sofa after  Christmas dinner and choosing their favourite  Quality Street or Roses to indulge in.  

Your Mum will probably go for the fruity cream  centre, your brother the fudge, your Granny  the Turkish delight, maybe your aunt will  experiment and try something new … They  will all go through different thought processes  before they pick out their chocolate and they all  have different cravings to satisfy. 

Consider how this is very much like a customer:  you have separate customers with separate  needs and they will all have an individual focus.  You have to be prepared for that. 

Your options are: 

a) Be totally bespoke and flexible so you  can provide services to anyone and everyone.  In this case you are not just the entire box of  chocolates but all the other boxes of chocolates  on the shelf. This is a broad brush approach and  requires a lot of one-to-one time to analyse each  potential client and create something unique  for them. 

OR 

b) Reduce the choice of chocolates (services)  and focus on just selling to Mum, Granny or  

*about the author*   
*Business Strategy* 

brother. This way you know what they want  and you know you can deliver it. You are much  more focused and can easily communicate this  to encourage them to hire you. 

Either approach can be successful but you need  to be clear which you are pursuing. 

I would suggest that you’d make life easier by  not trying to be the entire box of chocolates. I  know it’s tempting because you feel you’ll miss  out if you don’t offer something that someone  could potentially want but consider it from a  different angle… 

What if you were to hone in on certain key  services that you know you are great at and  work out who will love them – then focus on  selling to them specifically? 

This way: 

(i) You won’t feel as bad when someone  doesn’t pick you because you were never  compatible anyway 

(ii) You don’t waste time and effort trying to  attract the wrong people 

(iii) You get a lot of love from those who do  really like what you offer 

(iv) You end up with a customer who really  values you and is prepared to pay the right price 

**Creating Your Menu** 

Streamlining what you offer can help attract  more clients because it’s easier for them to  match their needs with your services. 

By packaging up your services you have a  selection of ready-made options which makes  the buying process so much easier. Potential  clients can scan and see immediately you have  what they want. 

**Whetting the Appetite** 

A key element in having success is costing  and pricing. You must make sure that you sit  at the right place in the market and that you  communicate this clearly.  

Packaging up your services ticks these boxes  and also puts you much more in control of your  profit.  

It’s so easy to under quote on a service based  task – you underestimate the time it takes, then  you throw a few extra hours in for ‘good will’  and before you know it you’ve spent more time  than you’re billing for, making your hourly rate  much less profitable. 

If you work out, based on a real example, how  long each type of task takes on average and  use that as a benchmark then you have a base  rate to work with. This will save you hours as  rather than quoting from scratch each time,  you already have the basis of your pricing  determined. 

Once you’re clear on your pricing you can then  get on with confidently selling your services to  potential clients knowing that it’s easy for them  to grasp what’s on offer. If it suits them then  it should be an easy sale. If it doesn’t it will  be quick to see and neither of you waste any  more time. 

**Enjoy Sweet Success** 

Chocolate companies are as wide and varied  as VAs. Chocolate brands offer a selection of  options, with an easy to understand menu and  so could VAs. Brands price themselves to attract  a certain kind of customers, so could VAs. 

To the ladies at Office\* who were feeling a bit  frustrated, I hope my musings might help you  see things differently and help you find a sense  of direction that removes some of the worry. 

The life of a VA should be dynamic and diverse.  The nature of the job means it will always be  varied. But if you can be as clean cut as possible  with the services you provide it will make  it easier to attract the right clients...and if  you need to work your way through a box of  chocolates to make sense of it all, well, so be it  and enjoy\! 

Claire Lister is MD at Pitman Training. Pitman Training run a range of courses around marketing and communications  and has just recently launched a course on pricing, helping service providers understand the value of their time. For  more information visit *www\.pitman-training.com*

*Communication* 

**Don’t Make Me  Think\!** 

**Get Your Website Organised In A Way That Makes Sense To  Your Potential Clients (So They Don’t Have To Think\!)** 

WRITER   
Marie Allen

*Don’t Make Me Think* is the title of one of the  classic books on web design, written by Steve  Krug in 2006\. Its premise is that website  visitors are in a hurry and don’t want to have to  spend time making choices and decisions.  

Human beings are inherently lazy and if  something seems difficult we are more than  likely to ditch it and move on\! Website  visitors want to be taken by the hand and  guided through a website to reach their desired  outcome. They don’t want to be left stranded,  unable to figure out what they should do next. 

How do you feel when you hit upon a website  that’s a bit of a muddle, where you can’t work  out where to go for the information you are  looking for? Where you get stuck in a dead-end  and can’t find a way back to where you started?  Annoyed? Frustrated? Websites like this are  one reason why the ‘Back’ key is the most used  key on the keyboard\! 

Within a few seconds you are off to the next  website on your search results list. 

So how do you ensure that this doesn’t happen  with your website? 

The key, as with so many things, is good  organisation. Organising your website so  that it is easy for your website visitors to find  their way around is not a quick and easy task  but, if you do it successfully it will really pay  dividends in terms of website conversions and  happy clients. 

When someone visits your website for the first  time they are generally looking for a specific  

*about the author* 

*![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()![]()*

product or service. You need to organise  your information from your potential client’s  perspective, not yours, so they find what they  want quickly. 

You are an expert in what you do, living and  breathing your business but your clients are  not. You need to get inside their heads and  work out how they think. Then you can get  ahead of them so that they don’t have to think  when they visit your site. This theory of putting  yourself in your clients’ shoes applies across all  of your marketing but it is particularly relevant  for websites because it is so easy to bail out and  move on to the next one. 

How do your existing clients categorise the  services you offer? What words do they use  to describe the services they buy from you?  Have a look at past emails and think about  conversations you had with your clients when  they first came to you. What were the areas  of confusion? Think it through and work out    
how you can make your website answer their  questions, without them having to think\! 

Try to replicate your clients’ language, as far  as you can, on your website. If you use the  same words that your potential clients use, not  industry-speak or techno-babble, they will feel  at home and are more likely to stick around and  buy from you. 

Getting your website to pass the “Don’t Make  Me Think” test 

The best way to pass the “Don’t Make Me  Think” test is to get the structure that you  come up with checked out by a variety of  people who are not directly involved with your  business. This can be done as a paper exercise,  using a separate sheet of paper or index card for  each web page.  

Get your friends and neighbours and some  existing clients to “test drive” your site  structure. You will be amazed at the number  of different ways in which different people’s  minds work. I can guarantee there will be some  tweaks needed for things you would never have  thought about\! There will be some difficult  decisions to make, but if you keep the client’s  perspective at the front of your mind you  cannot go too far wrong. 

The great news is that with a robust, client friendly website structure in place, your website  will convert more visitors and win you more  business. Your website can be an oasis of calm  in the busy world of your target audience – an  ordered place where they don’t have to think\!  

Marie Allen is Principal Consultant at Web Clarity, a company which helps small and medium-sized businesses get  maximum benefit from their website. She specialises in writing for the web and in creating a great user experience for  website visitors. Her e-book *25 Web Writing Hints and Tips to Win You More Customers\!* is available from the Web Clarity  website *www\.webclarity.co.uk*

*A to Z of Virtual Assistant Tools* 

**An A to Z of  VirtualAssistant  Tools – D is for  Dropbox**

WRITER   
Helen   
Stothard 

So that I can introduce you to more new ideas, I  try to keep the tools I share in the VA Magazine  different to those that appear in my book *A to  Z of Virtual Assistant Tools.* However, there are  just some tools that are so integral a part of my  Virtual Assistant business that they have to be  repeated here. 

Dropbox is a file sharing service that allows you  to store and share files and folders on the web  for collaborating with clients. It works in the  background, syncing files over the internet and  allows you to log in and access files from any  computer with an internet connection using  your secure user name and password. 

![]()  
Dropbox can be used as a form of backup,  especially if you pay a little extra for the ability  to restore deleted files. I’ve previously used  

various online back up and sync tools and  found that they just slowed my machine down.  Dropbox doesn’t make getting a new laptop  (I wear mine out roughly every 18 months) a  chore. I just download the Dropbox application  and off I go. 

I first came across Dropbox when I started  my Virtual Assistant business and I didn’t  like it. I wanted to specify where the files  and folders were stored on my computer and  not have all the shared files and folders in one  main Dropbox folder. It’s how I was taught to  store my information after losing three years of  work at an old employer’s when a new software  update deleted my user profile and therefore all  the files I had stored in My Documents. Since  then nothing was stored in My Documents on  my computer. I created a file on my C: Drive  and everything lived there so that if a user  profile was corrupted all the files were still  accessible from the hard drive. 

But I had an epiphany when setting up a new  laptop. I stored the Dropbox folder on my C  Drive and now use that for everything. I store  my files and folders exactly how I want, just  under the Dropbox instead of a folder I created;  it makes my life so much simpler.   
You can have a mixture of shared and unshared  folders in Dropbox. When working with a  new client I create a Dropbox folder in their  company name and share this with them. I then  have sub-folders within their company folder  where I can sub-divide the information. This  is particularly useful as it means we can both  access important information even if the other  party is offline. I use this a lot when working  with images and website content for clients. It  is also an efficient way of sharing large files that  would not fit into an email. 

However, when you’re sharing folders on  Dropbox you must remember that you cannot  share a subfolder with someone if you have  shared the main folder with someone else. It  might be the case that you are working on a  project with a client who wants a third party  to have access to just some of the information  you are storing. If this is the case you need to  share each sub folder individually or set up a  new folder that you grant access to. 

I think one of the most important lessons I  learned is that you have to have clear guidelines  on how you use your Dropbox when you share  your folders. I found out the hard way that not  everyone uses Dropbox for file storage, they just  use it to share files on a temporary basis and  

*www\.thevamagazine.com* 

forty five 

then delete the files when they have finished  with them. And yes, unless you pay extra to be  able to retrieve deleted files, once it’s gone, it’s  gone. I now have a conversation when I share a  Dropbox folder and explain how we will use it.  If I am sending work to an associate I copy and  paste it into the Dropbox folder, ensuring that  my master copy remains in the client folder,  then copy and paste the completed work back  into the client folder. 

A lot of clients think that they need a Virtual  Assistant to work in their office as it’s the only  way the Virtual Assistant can access the files  that they need. This isn’t the case when you  work with Dropbox. I have clients for whom  I have set Dropbox up to use in the same way  I do; it’s the main storage for all their files and  this means that we can both access the files we  need, wherever we are in the world, as long as  we have an internet connection. 

I recently needed a couple of rather large files  for a book interior and book cover from a client.  His designer had been using a file sharing site  which for some reason wasn’t working properly  on the day we urgently needed to complete the  project; it showed the files as being available  but refused to allow access to download them.  The solution was to create a folder that I  shared with the designer and the files were  available on my laptop in a fraction if the time  the file sharing application used to take. The  project was completed to the deadline and the  designers are now considering using Dropbox  themselves. 

I’m often told that I get all this functionality  because I pay for it, and yes, I do. My personal  Dropbox account costs me around £20 month,  and you’d be amazed at just how much is stored  in there. But let’s put this into perspective.  For £20 month all my files are backed up to  the internet; for £20 month I can access my  client’s files as though I was sat in his office  at his machine; for £20 month I had no hassle  when I upgraded my laptop, and for that same  £20 month I can access my files wherever I am  and whenever I want\! To me that’s excellent  value, and if you’re going to run your Virtual  Assistant business professionally then there  are certain tools you should invest in. To me  Dropbox is one of the main ones, and you may  find you don’t need as much storage as me and  your investment will be less. 

Dropbox is available in mobile applications,  which means that I can access the information  from my phone, tablet, netbook, laptop  or indeed any machine with an internet  connection. Even if I am out and about I can  share files and information with clients, which    
*A to Z of Virtual Assistant Tools* 

I couldn’t do if they were only stored on one  machine. 

If I am out and about and need to send a client  a file that isn’t already shared with them then  I simply open the Dropbox application on my  phone, (it’s password protected on the phone by  the way), and locate the file which I can then  email directly to them. It’s like having your  office with you all the time. It also makes you  incredibly efficient being able to work in this  manner. 

![]()  
You can also save favourite files to your Dropbox  phone application so that you can access them  when you don’t have an online connection. 

![]()

One of the new features on the Dropbox phone  application is the ability to sync photos and  videos from your phone direct to your Dropbox.  I don’t know about you, but I very rarely carry  a camera these days and use my phone if I  need to take photos so it’s great to have them  automatically backed up. 

Another option that those of you with  WordPress websites will like is the WordPress  plug in that allows you to back up your website  direct to your Dropbox. If you’ve ever lost a  website and found that your hosting company  doesn’t store backups then you’ll really  appreciate this feature.  

Dropbox just sits in the icon tray on your  computer and works in the background, it  checks to see what files you have amended or  a client has amended and syncs them. There is  a small pop up that will let you know when  new files are being received but after you have  been using it a while you don’t always notice  it’s there, so it’s advisable to ask clients to  contact you if they add any urgent work to your  Dropbox for you. 

![]()  
If you right click on the Dropbox icon in your  system tray you can open your Dropbox folder,  open the Dropbox website, view recently  changed files, see how much storage space  you have left, see how many files are currently  syncing, pause syncing (I occasionally do this  if I am doing a Skype video call to preserve as  much of my wireless bandwith as possible as I  have a very poor Skype connection, just don’t  forget to switch it back on from this system  tray dialogue again), amend your preferences or  visit the Dropbox help centre. 

I dragged a shortcut to the Dropbox folder, and  even some of the sub folders, to the favourites in  my Windows Explorer to make it even quicker  to access the files that I access most often.

forty six   
*www\.thevamagazine.com*   
*A to Z of Virtual Assistant Tools* 

To share a folder with someone from your computer simply right click on the folder and select the Dropbox option, then the share this folder option.  ![]()  
This will open up the Dropbox website and prompt you for the email address of the person you would like to share with. You can also personalise the  message that you send depending on how you wish to use the Dropbox with your Client. 

![]()  
Dropbox offers a free 2GB account for you to  start off with, and offers lots of opportunities  for you to increase your space at no cost to you,  from linking it to your Facebook account to  sharing it on Twitter or with friends. Signing  up on *http\://db.tt/KGePYoNe* will give you an  extra 500MB of free space on top of the 2GB  

*about the author*   
you would normally get (and it gives me an  extra 1GB as the referrer) or you can just sign  up at *http\://www\.dropbox.com* for the standard  2GB free account.  

Signing up for a free account is no risk, and I’d  definitely advise you try it out. You may find    
out that, as it did for me, it revolutionises the  way you run your Virtual Assistant business,  allowing you more flexibility in when and  where you work, and how quickly you can  access that important piece of information.
